- **Notices.** `core/command/policy-review/notices.mjs` composes the language notices with `coChangeNotices` (`history/co-change/notices.mjs`) when `history.coChange` is on and the analyzer is not disabled. `runQualityDiff` calls `describeNotices(changedFiles, { mergeBaseSha, touchedPaths })`: `touchedPaths` (`readTouchedPaths` in `core/diff/git/patches/project-moves.mjs`) holds every path of the base-to-head name-status diff, both sides of a rename, deletions and additions included, scoped to the project, and `coChangeNotices` reads the history at `mergeBaseSha` and resolves those paths against the merge base's files, so a deleted or renamed partner counts as touched. A changed module gets one notice for the partners no touched path belongs to and with which it shares at least `minConfidence` of its own commits (`support / commits(changed module)`, printed as a percentage), at most three partners by support and at most three modules by support, then a count of the rest; pairs lifted by `exceptions` are left out. Unavailable evidence is one notice with the reason (`unavailableNotice`); `historyAvailabilityNotes` writes it to stderr for `--all` runs, which have no notices.

## Facts schema (`shared/rules/facts/`)

The reuse, doc-comment, test-only-export and console rules are written once, over neutral facts. A language only produces the facts; `sharedFactFindings` turns them into findings. Lines are 1-based, a field marked optional may be left out, and unknown fields are ignored, so a producer can add its own. Every path is project-relative with `/` separators, with no leading `./`, no `.` or `..` segment, no empty segment and no trailing `/` (`app/orders/service.py`, never `./app/orders/service.py` or `app/orders/`); `validateFacts` rejects any other form.

A facts bundle is one JSON object. Each list is optional in the schema, but a rule needs the lists it reads (see the table below): when the bundle lacks one, `sharedFactFindings` throws instead of skipping the rule, so a registered rule without a producer is a tool error rather than a silent pass.

```json
{
  "modules": [
    { "file": "app/orders/service.py", "role": "production", "entry": false },
    { "file": "app/billing/invoice.py", "role": "production" }
  ],
  "packages": [{ "root": "libs/money", "published": true }],
  "definitions": [
    {
      "file": "app/orders/service.py",
      "name": "total",
      "kind": "function",
      "line": 12,
      "exported": true,
      "tokens": ["def", "total", "(", "items", ")", ":", "return", "sum", "(", "items", ")"]
    }
  ],
  "imports": [
    {
      "from": "app/billing/invoice.py",
      "to": "app/orders/service.py",
      "names": [{ "name": "total", "alias": "order_total" }],
      "reexport": false
    }
  ],
  "docComments": [
    { "file": "app/orders/service.py", "line": 11, "name": "total", "lines": ["Sum of the items."] }
  ],
  "console": [
    {
      "file": "tests/test_service.py",
      "test": "test_total",
      "messages": ["WARNING: rate limit is 0"]
    }
  ]
}
```

- **`modules`**: every file the producer read. `role` is `production` or `test` (a test file, or a helper only tests load, such as a `test-support/` module or `conftest.py`). `entry` (optional, default false) marks a file loaded by path rather than imported: a script, a `__main__` module, a class the build or container starts. When `modules` is present, every `imports[].from`, `imports[].to`, `definitions[].file` and `docComments[].file` must be one of its files, and every `console[].file` one of its files, a folder that holds one, or `""`; a dangling one is an error, so list the file or leave the entry out (an import of a file outside the analysed sources, such as a generated or excluded module, is left out).
- **`packages`**: `root` is the folder of a unit that is versioned and depended on by itself (an npm workspace package, a Gradle or Maven module, a Python distribution; `""` is the project root). A file belongs to the longest root that contains it. `published` says whether other projects install it (npm without `private`, `maven-publish`/`java-library` or a deployed Maven module, a `pyproject.toml` with `[project]` and a build backend and no `Private :: Do Not Upload` classifier). A published package's own tests count as callers, so `test-only-export` does not judge it. The package is also the unit `duplicate-definition`, `exported-name-collision` and `sibling-copy-edit` compare within. A single-package project passes `[]`.
- **`definitions`**: module-level declarations. `kind` is one of `function`, `class`, `const`, `type`, `interface`, `enum`. Map each language's declarations onto them:

  | Declaration                                                                       | `kind`      |
  | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------- |
  | Java `record`, Kotlin `object` and `data class`, Python `class`                   | `class`     |
  | Java `@interface`, Kotlin `typealias`, Python `Protocol` and type alias           | `type`      |
  | Java or Kotlin `interface`                                                        | `interface` |
  | Java static method, Kotlin top-level or `object` `fun`, Python `def`              | `function`  |
  | Java `static final`, Kotlin top-level or `object` `val`, Python `UPPER = literal` | `const`     |
  | Java and Kotlin `enum class`, Python `Enum` subclass                              | `enum`      |

  `exported` says whether another module can import it (TypeScript `export`; Python a public name or one in `__all__`; Java and Kotlin anything but `private`, since package-private Java code is usable from its package). `tokens` are the declaration's token texts in order, with comments and layout dropped and each string literal one token; two copies are compared by these tokens. A language whose blocks are set by indentation (Python) keeps them as `INDENT` and `DEDENT` tokens, so two bodies that differ only in nesting differ in tokens. A declaration with no tokens is never a copy. Emit the declarations of every module; those of a `test` module are ignored. Optional fields:
  - `qualifier` (default: `name`): the declaration's identity when its name is not unique in its file, such as `Money.of(int)` for one of several overloads or same-named methods of different classes. Findings, the base copy `sibling-copy-edit` compares with and the definitions an import resolves to are keyed by it; copies are still grouped by `name`.
  - `lines` (default 1): the declaration's line count. `duplicate-definition` leaves a declaration of 5+ lines and 50+ tokens to the duplicates check.
  - `size` (default: the number of tokens): the token count the duplicates check would see, for the 50-token cut-off.
  - `literal` (default false): a `const` whose value is built only from literals; only those `const`s are compared.
  - `usedLocally` (default false): the module itself reads the name outside its declaration. A name that is neither used locally nor imported by a production module is not compared (the dead-code check owns it).
  - `exportNames` (default `[name]` when `exported`): the names other modules import it by, for example `default` or an alias.
  - `key` (default: `text`): the identity two same-named copies must share to be duplicates; a producer that resolves free names adds them, so `return helper()` over two different `helper`s is not a copy.
  - `text` (default: the tokens joined by spaces): compared with the base copy to tell an edited declaration from an untouched one.
  - `entry` (default false): a framework entry (a route handler, a default export a framework loads); `sibling-copy-edit` skips it.
  - `overloads` (default false): the language picks among same-named declarations by their parameters (Java and Kotlin functions), so a module that imports two of them has overloads, not a name collision; `exported-name-collision` skips it.
  - `package` (default: from `packages`): overrides the package the declaration is compared within.

- **`imports`**: one per resolved dependency edge between two project files: an import statement, and also an implicit reference the language resolves without one, such as a class in the same Java or Kotlin package, or the parent `__init__.py` files a Python import runs (with an empty `names`). Edges to other packages or installed libraries are left out. `names` holds `{ name, alias? }` pairs: `name` is the name in the target, `alias` the local name when it differs. `*` is the whole module: a namespace import, `import pkg.mod`, a dynamic import, a star re-export, a Java wildcard `import com.acme.money.*` or Kotlin `import com.acme.money.*`, a Python `from mod import *`. A wildcard over a package becomes one edge per file of the package the importer uses, each with `[{ "name": "*" }]`, or, when the producer resolves which names it uses, one edge per file with those names. An empty list only loads the module. `reexport` (default false) marks a statement that makes the names part of the importer's own exports (`export { a } from`, `export *`, a Python `__init__.py` import of a name in `__all__` or of a public name). An edge's role is the role of its `from` module: an import from a `test` module is a test use, so it never keeps a name alive for `test-only-export`, `duplicate-definition` or `shared-used-by-one-feature`.
- **`docComments`**: one per doc comment on a declaration. `name` is the declaration's name and `qualifier` (optional, as for definitions) tells two same-named ones in one file apart; `lines` are the description lines: the text before the first block tag (`@param`, `@return` in JSDoc, Javadoc and KDoc; `:param x:`, `Args:`/`Returns:` sections or a numpy section underline in docstrings), with comment markers stripped and blank lines dropped. `detailed` (optional, default false) is true when what follows the description says more, and then the comment is never reported as restating its name: a block tag with text once the tag, a `{type}` and the name of a `@param`-style tag (or the exception class of a `@throws` without a `{type}`) are taken off, the metadata tags `@author`, `@since`, `@version`, `@see`, `@serial`, `@serialField` and `@serialData` never counting, as `tagsHaveContent()` reads it for JSDoc, Javadoc and KDoc, or a docstring section, field or example with text. The long check counts only prose: `codeExampleState()` in `shared/rules/doc-comments/doc-comments.mjs` skips fenced blocks (` ``` ` or `~~~` lines and what lies between; the last fence of an odd count is never closed, so it and what follows count as prose, and `descriptionLines()` still cuts at a block tag after it) and an `@example` tag with its body up to the next block tag (tag lines never count), for every language, so a producer may pass those lines through; bullets and other prose count.
- **`console`**: what tests printed, one entry per test, or per file or module when the runner only knows that. `file` is the test file; a runner that only knows the module, such as a Gradle or Maven module's test task output, gives the module folder (`""` for the root module) and `test: ""`. `test` is the test name (`""` when unknown), `messages` the printed lines that count: stderr lines, `console.error`/`console.warn` output, and stdout lines whose log level is `WARN` or `ERROR`. Normalization (paths, numbers, ids, ANSI colors) is done by the evaluator. `testConsole.allow[].paths` and the `test-console-output` exception paths match this `file` (`.` for a root module); a module folder is matched as it is, so lift a whole module with its folder (`billing`), not `billing/**`.

**Registering a rule.** `FACT_RULE_LANGUAGES` in `shared/project-rules/fact-rules.mjs` is the single source for which language enforces each fact rule: per language the value is `true`, `'partial'` or `false`. It fills the rule's columns of the checks table (the catalog in `shared/policy.mjs` gives no `enforcedBy` for these rules), decides which rules `sharedFactFindings` runs for a language, lets every preset name a rule a non-TypeScript language registers in `exceptions`, and widens that preset's config: `FACT_RULE_SETTINGS` names the config keys each rule reads (`testConsole` for `test-console-output`, `layout.sharedRoots` and `layout.featureRoots` for `shared-used-by-one-feature`), and a preset accepts those keys only once its language registers the rule. A runner calls `sharedFactFindings(facts, { language, analyzerName, policy, baseline, rules, siblingCopiesAcrossPackages })` from `shared/rules/facts/evaluate.mjs` and adds the findings it returns to its own; the TypeScript structure runner does the same for its three reuse rules (`structure/reuse/reuse-findings.mjs`):

- `policy` is the head policy (`readHeadPolicyFile()` from `shared/head-policy.mjs`, which every analyzer process can read). Its `exceptions`, `testConsole.allow` and `layout.sharedRoots`/`layout.featureRoots` apply. A policy of the wrong shape (including a `testConsole` or `layout` that is not an object) is a `TypeError` that names the field, and so is an `analyzerName` that is not a non-empty string.
- `baseline` is `{ facts, movesFile }` on the head run of a change: the bundle the producer emits for the base checkout (`--baseline-root`; only `definitions` are read) and a JSON file of `[basePath, headPath]` renames, which the TypeScript quality-area runners and the Java and Kotlin runners receive as `--baseline-moves-file`; the Python runner takes neither until a rule it registers reads the base. A runner that reads the base lazily passes `{ readFile }` instead: a function from a head file to `{ unchanged, definitions }`, as the TypeScript structure runner does. Without a baseline `sibling-copy-edit` reports nothing. Any other baseline value is a `TypeError`, and a bad baseline bundle fails like the head facts with `baseline` in front of the message.
- `rules` (optional) narrows the run to some registered rules, for a runner that produces some lists in one place and the rest in another (console output from test reports, definitions from source). A rule the language does not register, an empty list and a language that registers no rule at all are errors, never an empty result.
- `siblingCopiesAcrossPackages` (default false) lets `sibling-copy-edit` compare copies in different packages, such as the same class in two Gradle or Maven modules.
- `validateFacts` (`shared/rules/facts/schema.mjs`) checks the shapes, paths and module references (when `modules` is given, every import end, definition and doc comment names a listed file, and every console entry a listed file, a folder holding one, or the root) first and names the first bad field; `readFactsFile(file)` reads a producer's JSON output and validates it.

| Rule                         | Lists it reads                                                  |
| ---------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `duplicate-definition`       | `modules`, `packages`, `definitions`, `imports`                 |
| `exported-name-collision`    | `modules`, `packages`, `definitions`, `imports`                 |
| `sibling-copy-edit`          | `modules`, `packages`, `definitions` (head; base `definitions`) |
| `shared-used-by-one-feature` | `modules`, `imports`                                            |
| `test-only-export`           | `modules`, `packages`, `definitions`, `imports`                 |
| `doc-comment-content`        | `docComments`                                                   |
| `test-console-output`        | `console`                                                       |

**Feature layout.** `shared-used-by-one-feature` needs to know which folders are shared and which are features. `layout.sharedRoots` and `layout.featureRoots` are folder globs (a Java or Kotlin package is its source folder, such as `**/com/acme/shared`). A file under a shared root is shared; a file under `<feature root>/<name>/` belongs to feature `<name>`. The TypeScript default is `<source root>/main/shared` and `<source root>/main/features` (the folder names `architecture/strict-layout.mjs` shares with the import boundary rules, which do not follow the config keys); the other presets default to `**/shared` and `**/features`. Without a feature folder the rule finds nothing.

## Design facts (`shared/rules/design/`)

The deep-module design signals are written once, over two more lists of the facts bundle: `types`
and `callables`. A language produces them from its own parser; `sharedFactFindings` runs the rules.
Every path follows the facts path rules, and every `types[].file` and `callables[].file` must be a
file of `modules`. Only production modules are judged; facts of `test` modules may be left out
(when present they count as test callers, which never make a finding). Generated files (the
generated-code policy of the language) are left out of `modules`, `types` and `callables`.

Ids are opaque strings, unique within their list. Use `<file>#<qualified name>` (for example
`src/orders/cart.ts#Cart`, `src/orders/cart.ts#Cart.add`, `app/orders/cart.py#total`); overloads
add the parameter count or types (`Money.java#Money.of(int)`), and a module's own entry is its file.
A reference to an id that is not in its list is an error, so only emit references the producer
resolved: an unresolved call is not listed in `calls`, it makes the uses it touches `opaque`.

```json
{
  "types": [
    {
      "id": "src/orders/cart.ts#Cart",
      "file": "src/orders/cart.ts",
      "name": "Cart",
      "line": 3,
      "kind": "class",
      "exported": true,
      "fields": [{ "name": "items", "mutable": true, "private": true }]
    }
  ],
  "callables": [
    {
      "id": "src/orders/cart.ts#Cart.lines",
      "file": "src/orders/cart.ts",
      "name": "lines",
      "line": 9,
      "kind": "method",
      "owner": "src/orders/cart.ts#Cart",
      "exported": true,
      "params": [],
      "returnsField": "items"
    },
    {
      "id": "src/checkout/pay.ts#pay",
      "file": "src/checkout/pay.ts",
      "name": "pay",
      "line": 4,
      "kind": "function",
      "owner": "src/checkout/pay.ts",
      "exported": true,
      "params": [{ "name": "cart", "type": "src/orders/cart.ts#Cart" }],
      "calls": [
        {
          "target": "src/orders/cart.ts#Cart.lines",
          "line": 5,
          "receiver": "cart",
          "mutation": "push"
        }
      ]
    }
  ]
}
```

- **`types`**: the project's own types and modules that own members. `kind` is one of:

  | `kind`      | What it is                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
  | ----------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
  | `class`     | a class with behaviour: TypeScript/JavaScript, Java, Kotlin and Python classes that declare methods                                                                                                                                                            |
  | `data`      | a type that only carries values: a TypeScript interface or object type, a Java `record`, a Kotlin `data class`, a Python dataclass, `TypedDict` or pydantic model without methods, any class with no methods besides accessors, `equals`/`hashCode`/`toString` |
  | `interface` | a contract other types implement: a Java or Kotlin `interface`, a TypeScript interface that a class `implements`, a Python `Protocol` or ABC                                                                                                                   |
  | `module`    | a file seen as the owner of its top-level functions and state (TypeScript, JavaScript, Python modules, a Kotlin file's top-level functions)                                                                                                                    |
  | `enum`      | an enum, or a union of literals (TypeScript `'a' \| 'b'`, Python `Literal[...]`); `cases` lists its members                                                                                                                                                    |

  `name` is the declared name (a module's is its file name without extension). `exported` says
  whether other modules can use it (the definitions rule: TypeScript `export`, Python a public name,
  Java and Kotlin `public`, `protected` or `internal`; a module is always exported). Optional fields:
  - `fields` (default `[]`): the state the type owns. `name`; `mutable` (default false) when the
    owner creates the value as a mutable collection or object and never replaces it with a copy
    (`[]`, `{}`, `new Map()`, `new ArrayList<>()`, `mutableListOf()`, `list()`, `dict()`, `set()`);
    `private` (default false) when no other module can reach the field itself (TypeScript
    `private`/`#field` or a module-level `const` that is not exported, Java and Kotlin `private`,
    Python a leading `_`). A `data` type lists every property; a `class` its instance fields; a
    `module` its top-level variables.
  - `cases` (enums only): the member names, in declaration order (string literals without quotes).
  - `supertypes` (default `[]`): the ids of the project types this type directly extends or
    implements; library supertypes are left out.
  - `published` (default false): the type is on the public surface of a published package, so
    projects outside this one may call it. For npm, a declaration the package's entry points export
    (the public surface `test-only-export` uses, under `published` in `packages`); for a published
    Gradle or Maven module or Python distribution, every exported type of it. A member of a
    published type counts as published too.
  - `references` (`module` types only, default `[]`): the resolved project callables that
    module-level code calls or uses as values (a shape table, a registration at load time), so a
    module whose only uses of another module sit outside any function still counts as its consumer.

- **`callables`**: functions, methods and constructors with a body, in production modules and
  (optionally) test modules. `kind` is `function`, `method`, `constructor` or `accessor` (a getter
  or a Kotlin property getter). `owner` is the id of the `types` entry that declares it (a class, or
  the module for a top-level function); leave it out for a local function. `exported` says whether
  another module can call it: an exported top-level function, or a public (`public`, `protected`,
  `internal`, Python without a leading `_`) member of an exported type. Optional fields:
  - `entry` (default false): the framework or the language calls it, not the project: an override
    or an implementation of an interface member, a decorated or annotated function (`@Get`,
    `@Transactional`, `@app.route`, `@Bean`, `@Override` aside from `toString`/`equals`), a `main`,
    a callback the producer sees passed to a framework registration. Entries are never judged as
    forwarding hops, data clumps or context owners.
  - `published` (default false): as for `types`, for a top-level function on the public surface
    of a published package.
  - `references` (default `[]`): the resolved project callables this callable uses as values
    without calling them: passed as a callback, stored, returned, or held in a module-level
    constant (a dispatch table) that the callable reads, one level deep. `disjoint-consumer-groups`
    counts them as uses, like `calls`.
  - `params` (default `[]`): `{ name, type? }` in declaration order. `type` is the id of a `types`
    entry when the declared (or, for TypeScript, checked) type resolves to one, otherwise the
    type's normalized text with whitespace removed and without generic arguments of project types
    (`string`, `int`, `List`); leave `type` out when the parameter has no declared type. A
    variadic, a default value, or a destructuring pattern make `variadic: true`, `optional: true`
    or `pattern: true` (each default false).
  - `returns`: the return type, normalized like a parameter type; leave it out when not declared.
  - `forward`: set only when the whole body is one call of a resolved project callable that passes
    some parameters unchanged and returns its result unchanged (or the callable returns nothing and
    the call is the only statement): `{ target, arguments, returned }`. `arguments` holds, per
    argument of the call, the index of the parameter passed (`-1` for anything else: a literal, an
    expression, a field, `this`); `returned` says whether the call's result is the return value.
  - `calls` (default `[]`): every call of a resolved project callable, in source order, as
    `{ target, line, receiver?, cleanup?, branch?, mutation? }`. `receiver` is a key for the value the call
    is made on, the same key for every call on the same value within this callable: the
    parameter or local name, `this.<field>` (Python `self.<field>`) for a field of the owner, or
    `this` (Python `self`) for the owner itself; leave it out for a static or top-level call.
    `cleanup` is true when the call runs on every exit: in a `finally` block, a TypeScript `using`
    declaration's disposal, Python `with`, Java try-with-resources, Kotlin `use { }`. `branch`
    lists the arms of the conditionals the call sits in, as `"<conditional>:<arm>"` (the
    conditional's ordinal in the callable, the arm `then`, `else` or the index of a `case` or
    `when` entry); two calls with the same conditional and different arms never run in one
    execution, so `protocol-contract` does not count a step on one arm as running before an open
    on the other. Producers that leave it out make every call count as possibly running before the
    next. `mutation`
    names the first change the caller makes to the value the call returned, directly
    (`cart.lines().push(x)`) or through a local that holds it and is never reassigned
    (`const lines = cart.lines(); lines.push(x)`): the mutating method as the language names it
    (`push`, `add`, `put`, `set`, `delete`, `remove`, `clear`, `sort`, `splice`, `append`,
    `extend`, `update`, `pop`, Java's `addAll`, `putAll`, `removeIf`...), or `assign` for an element
    or property assignment (`lines[0] = x`, `config.timeout = 3`). The list is open: the rules only
    check that `mutation` is present, so each producer names the mutators of its own language and
    the message quotes them.
  - `paramUses` (default `[]`): how each parameter whose type is a project `class`, `data` or
    `interface` type is used: `{ param, fields, forwards, opaque }`. `param` is the parameter's
    index; `fields` the names of its members read or called (`ctx.clock`, `ctx.clock.now()` reads
    `clock`; a destructuring reads each name); `forwards` the calls that receive the whole value as
    `{ target, position }` (the argument position in that call); `opaque` (default false) is true
    when any other use exists: the value is returned, stored, spread, captured by a closure, read
    with a computed key, passed to an unresolved callable or to a resolved one at a position it
    does not declare. Opaque uses make the evidence incomplete, never "unused".
  - `ownFields` (default `[]`): the names of the owner's `fields` this callable reads or writes
    (`this.items`, `self._items`, a module-level variable).
  - `returnsField`: the name of a field of the owner that a `return` (or an expression body)
    returns itself, not a copy; leave it out when every return is a copy, a new value or another
    expression.
  - `decisions` (default `[]`): every branch on one subject compared with constants: a `switch`,
    `when` or `match` over it, or an `if`/`else if` chain (or `&&`/`||` condition) of equality tests
    (`===`, `==`, `equals`, `is`, `in` with a literal tuple or set of constants) on it.
    `{ subject, groups, line, otherwise }`: `subject` is the id of an `enum` entry when the tested
    value has that type, or `<type id>.<field>` when it is a field of a project type
    (`order.status` of `Order` gives `src/orders/order.ts#Order.status`); leave out decisions on
    other values. `groups` lists, per arm in source order, the constants that lead to it (enum
    member names or literal texts without quotes); `otherwise` (default false) says the decision
    has a `default`, `else` or wildcard arm.
  - `reads` (default `[]`): per value of a project `class` or `data` type other than the owner
    itself (a parameter, a local or a field of the owner): `{ type, fields, decided, line }`, with
    `fields` the members read on it, `decided` (default `[]`) those of `fields` that are part of a
    branch condition (`if`, `while`, a ternary, a `switch`/`when`/`match` subject, a `&&`/`||`
    operand of a condition), and `line` the first read.

### Design rules

All rules skip test modules and entries. The rules that judge the shape of an API
(`context-threading`, the discovery part of `caller-protocol`, the head of a `forwarding-chain`,
`disjoint-consumer-groups` and `data-clump`) also skip what is `published`, whose callers outside
the project are unknown; the other rules judge code whose callers the project holds, so a published
package does not change them. Each finding names the caller, the owner and the public
surface it is about, and says which smaller contract would remove it. Findings are report-only
(`descriptive`) unless the table says otherwise: these are review signals, not proofs of a design
defect, and their thresholds are explicit here.

| Rule                       | Lists it reads                  | Fires when                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| -------------------------- | ------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `mutable-state-escape`     | `modules`, `types`, `callables` | enforceable: a caller in another module mutates the value an exported member returned, and that value is a private mutable field of the member's owner                                            |
| `context-threading`        | `modules`, `types`, `callables` | a parameter of a type with 5 or more fields is passed whole through at least two callables, one in another module, and all of them together read at most a third of its fields (at least 2)       |
| `caller-protocol`          | `modules`, `types`, `callables` | the same ordered steps (3 or more members) on one value of a project class are repeated by 3 or more callables in 2 or more modules outside the class's module                                    |
| `protocol-contract`        | `modules`, `types`, `callables` | enforceable: a call breaks a `protocols` contract (an `open` member without a `close` member on every exit, or a listed step before `open`)                                                       |
| `forwarding-chain`         | `modules`, `types`, `callables` | two or more forwarding hops in a row, each into another module, pass the same parameters in order with the same parameter and return types                                                        |
| `disjoint-consumer-groups` | `modules`, `types`, `callables` | the exported members of one owner used from other modules split into 2 or more groups of 2 or more members that no caller module uses together and that share no field or helper inside the owner |
| `data-clump`               | `modules`, `types`, `callables` | 3 or more typed parameters (same names and types) appear together in 3 or more exported callables in 2 or more modules                                                                            |
| `repeated-policy-decision` | `modules`, `types`, `callables` | two decisions in different modules over the same subject group its constants the same way, with at least one arm covering 2 or more constants                                                     |
| `foreign-state-decision`   | `modules`, `types`, `callables` | 2 or more callables outside a `class` (one with behaviour) branch on the same 2 or more of its fields                                                                                             |

**Protocol contracts.** `protocols` in `nitpicker.config.json` lists the lifecycles a project wants enforced: `[{ "type": "Session", "open": ["begin"], "close": ["commit", "rollback"], "steps": ["write"] }]`. `type` is a `types[].name` (or id); `open` and `close` are required member names, `steps` optional. Calls are grouped by receiver alone, and a contract governs a receiver when its `type` is the owner of one of the receiver's call targets or a transitive `supertypes` entry of that owner, so a lifecycle split across a class hierarchy (an inherited `open`, an overriding `close`, a contract on an interface the class implements) is one lifecycle. A callable that calls an `open` member on a receiver must call a `close` member on the same receiver with `cleanup: true` (in `finally`, `with`, `use`, try-with-resources or a `using` disposal), and must not call a `steps` member on that receiver before its first `open`. Violations are enforceable `protocol-contract` findings at the `open` (or the early step); the sequences `caller-protocol` discovers stay report-only, so the catalog marks each rule with one flag. A contract is a reviewed decision, so the rule never infers a mandatory lifecycle from names. An open is not reported unclosed when the receiver is a field of the caller's owner (`this.<field>`, `self.<field>`: the object owns the lifecycle across its methods) or when the caller returns the contract type or a subtype of it (it hands the opened resource over), so `returns` must be the project type id like a parameter type. Each `type` appears once in `protocols` (config validation), so the loosening check compares contracts by `type`.

**Finding locations.** A finding that spans several modules is placed on its subject, so adding a caller in another file never moves an existing note: `caller-protocol` on the called class, `repeated-policy-decision` on the enum or the field's owner, `foreign-state-decision` on the class whose fields are read. `data-clump` has no subject and stays on the first site by path; its identity is the parameter set.

## TypeScript preset (`languages/typescript/`)

- **Strict preset assembly.** Flat config replaces rather than merges a rule's options when several entries match a file, so each scope in `eslint-config/strict.mjs` carries its full `no-restricted-syntax` list: the everywhere selectors plus the scope's own. Add a selector to its scope's list, never as a separate entry for a narrower glob. `domain/` has its own loop and forEach selectors, so it drops those from the production set. Default exports, `null` and promise chains are not in these lists: `nitpicker/no-default-export`, `nitpicker/no-null` and `nitpicker/no-promise-chain` (`eslint-plugin/rules/syntax/syntax-bans.mjs`) take the same `{ selector, message }` options as `no-restricted-syntax`, are set once on the source entry (domain/ and adapters/ inherit them, the config-file entry turns the default-export and promise-chain ones off) and in the test entry (promise chains), so an exception for one lifts only that ban. The JavaScript dialect strips TypeScript-only selectors from `nitpicker/no-null` as it does from `no-restricted-syntax`. `strictSyntaxConfig` (`strict.mjs`), `strictAdaptersConfig` (`strict-adapters.mjs`) and `strictTestsConfig` (`strict-tests.mjs`) are standalone configs so the failure-message harness can lint one rule family alone; no tool loads them by path, which is why they are ordinary modules and not `*.config.mjs` files. The typed ones (`strictConfig(parserOptions)`, `strictAdaptersTypedConfig`) need `parserOptions` (e.g. `{ project: './tsconfig.quality.json', tsconfigRootDir }`) and later entries win.
- **Immutability check.** `functional/prefer-immutable-types` is-immutable-type caches a verdict per recursion identity, and for a type that recurses through its own arguments (`readonly (readonly T[])[]`) the checker hands out one identity for every depth, so the verdict depended on which file was linted first. `type-identity/functional-plugin.mjs` wraps the rule so that, past 12 nested type arguments, instantiations share one identity and each shallower instantiation keeps its own (`recursion-identity.mjs` replaces the checker's internal `getRecursionIdentity` once per checker, and leaves a checker without that method alone; the replacement is shared by every typed rule on that program, which only changes how deep instantiations are told apart). Every config uses that one plugin object, because flat config rejects two objects under one plugin name.
- **Scripts in a TypeScript project.** `javascript-in-typescript-project` (`structure/sources/javascript-files.mjs`, run by `typescriptStructureProjectFindings`) reports every `.js`, `.mjs`, `.cjs` and `.jsx` file (`SCRIPT_EXTENSION`, derived from `codeExtensionNames`) of `layout.projectCodeFiles` that no exception for the rule covers and that is not a test input (`layout.isTestFixture`: under `fixtures/` or `__fixtures__/` in a test location), when the head checkout (`liveProjectRoot`) is a TypeScript project, that is it has a `tsconfig.json` outside `node_modules` and the exclusions (`projectTsconfigs`, which also follows `references`), and the preset is not `javascript`. `layout.entryPoints` do not lift it. The message names the `.ts` or `.tsx` file to write instead. The identity is the rule id, so the file path alone tells base and head apart and only an added file is new. The type-aware lint rules run on scripts as on TypeScript. The rule's id, message and catalog entry live in `shared/project-rules/source-rules.mjs` with `component-story-missing`, spread into `shared/policy.mjs`.
- **Built-in excludes.** `layout.mjs` anchors `dist`, `build`, `generated`, `coverage`, `reports` and `target` to the project root and `node_modules` to any depth (`EXCLUDED_PATH_PATTERN`, the one regular expression dependency-cruiser, the structure analyzer and the spawned-entry scan share). The JSON tool configs (`tsconfig.quality.json`, `tsconfig.stryker.json`, `jscpd.json`, `knip.json`) and `vitest.quality.config.ts` write the same folders, and `layout.test.mjs` keeps them equal to `builtInExcludedGlobs`. A `layout.excludedGlobs` entry that is not built in hides its files like `exclude` (`isExcluded`, `excludePattern`). Generated code is decided by content, not name: `markedGeneratedFiles` (`layout/generated-files.mjs`) keeps the tracked code files that `mayBeGenerated` (a `*.gen.*`, `*.generated.*`, `*.pb.*` or `*_pb.*` name, a `__generated__/` folder, or a root output folder) and whose first five lines match `GENERATOR_MARKER` (`@generated`, `DO NOT EDIT`, `<auto-generated`, `auto-generated`, `This file was generated`). `withProjectFacts` puts the list in the head policy as `generatedFiles`; `isGeneratedPath` (and so `isSourceFile`, `projectCodeFiles` and the reuse walk) and the outside-roots rule's `generatedFilePattern` read it, so an unmarked file is checked like any other. The default roots are `sourceRoots` and `testRoots` of `layout.mjs`, which the project checks, the base coverage and the tool configs import, and folder walks go through `filesUnder` (`shared/filesystem.mjs`). `*.d.ts` is not excluded: ESLint lints it and TypeScript reads its declarations (the analysis tsconfig sets `skipLibCheck`, so errors inside `.d.ts` files, the project's own included, are not reported; checking them would also check every package's types), and `runtimeExcludedGlobs` adds it for the tools that instrument code (Stryker, CRAP, coverage).
- **Lint errors that are not findings.** ESLint 9 reports `/* eslint-env */` as a fatal message and its runner used to throw on it; `withoutFatalDirectives` (`adapters/hints/eslint-hints.mjs`) drops that message in the three ESLint analyzers and the regular lint rules report the comment as one finding.
- **React scope.** `eslint.config.mjs` adds `reactScopeConfigFor()` and passes `{ react: true }` to `strictPresetConfig()` only when `isReactProject()` says so: the head policy's `react.enabled` when set, else `react` in the analyzed tree's `dependencies` or `devDependencies` (the head policy carries the decision: `resolveHeadPolicy(config, projectRoot)` fills `react.enabled` and `i18n.enabled` from the head checkout's manifest unless the config sets them, so base and head are linted with the same scope, a pull request that adds React or `react-intl` does not make every existing finding new, and the policy hash and cache key include the decision; a run without a project root, such as `--fix`, falls back to the manifest of the tree being linted, read from `NITPICKER_DIFF_ESLINT_PROJECT_ROOT`). The plugins load through a dynamic import, so a non-React run never pays for them. `react: true` changes one `strict-declarations.mjs` selector: classes extending `Component` or `PureComponent` leave the "classes only for `Error` subclasses" ban and belong to `react-x/no-class-component`. The head policy carries `react` only when the config sets it, which keeps zero-config cache keys unchanged. Unset `react.styling` is resolved once from the head checkout into the head policy (`stylingApproachFor()` in `eslint-config/react/react-styling.mjs`, called from `detectedScopes()`): `usedStylingApproach()` counts the layout's source files (at most 3000) that import a stylesheet or pass `className`, import a CSS-in-JS library (`cssInJsSource` in `layout.mjs`), call `StyleSheet.create` or write an inline `style` object, and takes the largest count (ties in that order); with none, `dependencyStylingApproach()` reads the manifest (`css-in-js`, `style-sheet` for `react-native` or `expo`, else `class-names`). Base and head are linted with the head's approach and the policy hash includes it. `react.autoCleanup` is resolved the same way (`testsCleanUpAutomatically()` in `scope-support.mjs`: true without a Vitest dependency, else true only when the project's Vitest config sets `globals: true`); the test entry turns `testing-library/no-manual-cleanup` off when it is false. The production and test entries carry `globals.browser` unless the tree lists `react-native` or `expo`, so a JavaScript React project knows `document`; `browserGlobalEntries()` adds `no-restricted-globals` with `restrictedBrowserGlobals` (the standard confusing-browser-globals list, each naming `window.<name>`) for the same files, as the last entry of `reactScopeConfigFor()` and ignoring the `domain/` globs, whose own `no-restricted-globals` list would otherwise be replaced. `isReactProject()` returns false for the JavaScript preset (`layout.javascript`) whatever the manifest or `react.enabled` says, and `validateConfig` rejects the `react` key with that preset, so there is no config route to switch the scope on or off there. With the scope off, `eslint.config.mjs` drops the exceptions that name a React rule id (`reactRuleIds`) before `applyRuleExceptions`, which still rejects unknown ids. With the scope on, `strict-escape-hatches.mjs` also lets `null` through as the argument and type argument of `useRef` and `createContext`. The custom React rules resolve `useEffect`, `useLayoutEffect` and `useState` through their `react` import bindings (aliases, namespace imports); an identifier with no binding at all is matched by name, so auto-imported hooks still count. `react-rules.mjs` lists every React rule id (strict, like the other scopes' rules); `react-scope.test.mjs` checks it against the config and the accessibility names against the plugin's recommended set. The test rules (`reactTestRuleIds`) are a second entry in `reactSyntaxConfigFor()`, scoped to test files (`layout.testGlobs` plus `__tests__` directories) and independent of the runner: `eslint-plugin-testing-library` (peer range includes ESLint 10) supplies `no-test-id-queries`, `no-node-access` (which also reports `container.querySelector`, so `no-container` is left out to avoid a second message), `prefer-user-event`, `prefer-screen-queries` (in the plugin's own React config; reports queries destructured from a `render` result), `await-async-events`, `await-async-queries`, `await-async-utils`, `prefer-user-event-setup`, `prefer-presence-queries`, `no-unnecessary-act`, `no-debugging-utils` and `no-manual-cleanup`, and `nitpicker/react-test-no-implementation-details` covers what the plugin does not (component instances and state, fiber internals, spies on React hooks; a spy target counts as React when it is `React`, `react`, a binding of the `react` package (default, `{ default as R }`, namespace, `import = require`, `require`, `await import('react')`, `vi.importActual('react')`, `jest.requireActual('react')`, wherever in the file it is declared) or an alias of one (`const R = React`, `.default`), or a direct load of `react`, resolved by `reactBindingNames()` and `isReactExpression()` in `react-test-files.mjs`; the hook name may carry `as 'useState'`). Upstream `prefer-user-event-setup` recognises a direct call only through the default `userEvent` import once `utils-module` is set, so `withNamedUserEventImport()` reports `userEvent.click(...)` (any method but `setup`) in a file whose only import of `@testing-library/user-event` is the named `{ userEvent }` (or an alias of it). `testingLibraryPlugin` wraps each upstream rule three ways. `withHint()` appends the allowed form. `withoutFixes()` drops `fix` and `suggest` from every report: ESLint 10 calls a fixer at report time, and upstream's `await-async-events` fixer sets `async = true` on the node, which crashed `@typescript-eslint/require-await` and with it the whole analyzer. `inReactTestFilesOnly()` (`eslint-plugin/rules/react-tests/react-test-files.mjs`, also used by the custom rule) runs the rule only in a `.tsx`/`.jsx` file that is not a browser end-to-end test (`isBrowserEndToEndTest()` in `analysis/test-code-analysis.mjs`: an `@playwright/*` or `cypress` import, or a path under the repo-root `e2e/` test root), a file that imports `@testing-library/*`, `react`, `react-*`, `enzyme` or a relative `test-utils` module, or a file that mocks `react` or `react-dom` with `vi.mock`/`jest.mock` (`mockedModuleSource()` in `analysis/test-code-analysis.mjs`, the one mock parser the testing rules share), because the plugin's own detection is aggressive without an import and would flag Playwright `getByTestId` or a zustand `setState` in a domain test. Otherwise it walks each import with `reachableModules()` (`modules/module-resolution.mjs`, so tsconfig `paths` and `baseUrl` apply; at most 4 imports deep and 50 modules, a truncated walk counts as no match) over `moduleSourcesOf()` (`modules/module-sources.mjs`: every import, export, `import =`, dynamic import and `require` source, scanned with the TypeScript parser and cached by file stamp), and counts a reached project module named `test-utils` (or an `index` file in a `test-utils` folder) or one that imports `@testing-library/*`, `enzyme` or `react-test-renderer`. Imports of a file whose name is not absolute are matched by text only. The test entry sets `testing-library/utils-module` to `test-utils` so renders re-exported from a project wrapper count as Testing Library renders; when a file reaches Testing Library only through a helper (no direct `@testing-library/*` import other than `user-event` and `jest-dom`), the wrapper hands the inner rule `context.settings` with `utils-module` set to that import's specifier, so the plugin treats the helper as the Testing Library module and reports its queries (the plugin follows one utils module per file, so a file that imports both keeps the official one). Its `state()` and `setState()` checks apply only to a wrapper from enzyme's `mount`/`shallow`/`render` or `react-test-renderer`'s `create` (a variable assigned from one, or the call itself), so a zustand `store.setState` passes. `withNamedUserEventImport()` also follows a namespace import (`UE.userEvent.click()`, `UE.default.click()`). The custom rule also reports `vi.mock`/`vi.doMock`/`jest.mock`/`jest.doMock` of `react` (any mock) or of `react-dom` when the factory has a `use*` key. The default `testRoots` are `test`, `tests`, `__tests__` and `e2e`, so the test entry and the other layout-driven tool configs cover repo-root `__tests__/` and `e2e/`; `vitest.quality.config.ts` leaves `e2e` out of its own default roots (it does not import the layout, being copied into the project), because an `e2e/` folder usually holds Playwright or Cypress specs that vitest cannot execute; a `layout.testRoots` entry is always honoured, and `layout.executedTestRoots` (default roots minus `e2e`) feeds `tsconfig.stryker.json`, which keeps `e2e` out of mutation. `layout.isEndToEndTest()` (repo-root `e2e/` only, when it is a test root) exempts those specs from the `test-runtime` analysis, and `isBrowserEndToEndTest()` (that path, or a Playwright or Cypress import anywhere) from `nitpicker/test-must-use-production-runtime`, and `defaults/eslint.config.mjs` lets `async ({}, use)` fixtures through `no-empty-pattern` on `layout.endToEndGlobs`. `withEndToEndDom()` in `tool-configs.mjs` and `projectLibs()` in `modules/tsconfig-files.mjs` add the `dom` lib to `tsconfig.quality.json` (the shipped one, or the lib TypeScript resolves from the project's tsconfig through `extends`) when the folder exists, so `page.evaluate(() => document...)` type-checks. `test-must-use-production-runtime` resolves imports with `resolveModuleSpecifier()` (so `paths`, `baseUrl` and workspace packages apply) and also accepts a helper whose runtime imports include production code, following the helper's re-exports with `reachableModules()` (3 levels, 50 modules) over `moduleReferencesOf()` (`modules/module-sources.mjs`, which marks each source as runtime or type-only and as a re-export; `import type`, `export type` and inline `type` specifiers do not count). `dependency-cruiser.config.mjs` and the production-module check of `test-must-use-production-runtime` anchor the test roots at the project root, so `src/crypto/e2e/` stays production. Snapshot-only tests have no rule on purpose. `reactScopeConfigFor()` turns `no-test-id-queries` off when the analyzed tree lists `@testing-library/react-native`. It also leaves out `react-x/no-forward-ref` and `react-x/no-context-provider` (`react19RuleIds`) when the React major is below 19, and passes `${major}.0.0` as the `react-x` version setting, so the plugins do not detect the version from the linted tree. The major is `react.major` in the head policy: `resolveHeadPolicy()` reads it once from the head checkout (`reactMajorOf()` in `scope-support.mjs`: `node_modules/react/package.json`, else the `react` range in `package.json`) when the scope is on, so base and head use the same version and the policy hash and cache key include it; without a policy value the linted tree's version is used, and an unreadable version keeps the rules.
- **Framework conventions.** `frameworkConventions()` in `scope-support.mjs` reads the head checkout once for `resolveHeadPolicy()` and writes `frameworks: { defaultExports, entryPoints }` into the head policy when it finds any: the Next.js route and `pages/` globs and `middleware` (`next` in the manifest or a `next.config.*`), `storyGlobs` (`@storybook/*`, `storybook` or a `.storybook/` folder), every file under `app/` or `src/app/` (`expo-router`), and the Vite entry (`vite` or a `vite.config.*`: the module `<script src>` paths of `index.html`, else the first existing `src/main.{tsx,ts,jsx,js}`). `strictSyntaxConfigFor()` turns `nitpicker/no-default-export` off for `frameworks.defaultExports` (`frameworkFileEntries()` in `strict.mjs`), and `readLayout()` appends `frameworks.entryPoints` to `layout.entryPoints`, so the module-load rule and the dead-code entries both see them.
- **React rule groups.** Besides the rules above, `eslint-config/react/rules/third-party-rules.mjs` holds one hint per `react-x`, `react-dom` and `react-web-api` rule the scope enables (`reactXHints`, `reactDomHints`, `reactWebApiHints`); `react-plugins.mjs` wraps each with `withHint()` and `withoutFixes()` (the upstream fixers and suggestions are never run), and `thirdPartyReactRuleIds` feeds `reactRuleIds`. A rule that needs type information (`react-x/no-leaked-conditional-rendering`, listed in `typedReactXRules`) is configured in the typed entry of `reactScopeConfigFor()`, which is where `no-misused-promises` and `no-confusing-void-expression` (with `ignoreVoidReturningFunctions`, so a JSX handler may return a void call) live; the untyped test harness never loads it. The nitpicker rules for form fields, keys, direct DOM access and effect callbacks are in `eslint-plugin/rules/react/` (`forms/`, `render/`) and listed in `eslint-config/react/rules/nitpicker-rules.mjs`; the JSX helpers and field sets they share (`attributeNamed`, `fieldElements`, `buttonLikeInputTypes`, `fixedValueInputTypes`) are in `eslint-plugin/rules/react/jsx.mjs`. `react-no-effect-data-fetching` needs no type information: `ownerChain()` walks from a node up to the effect callback through functions the effect itself runs (a function declared in it and called by it, an IIFE, a callback passed to `setTimeout`, `setInterval`, `requestAnimationFrame` or a promise's `then`/`catch`/`finally`), and stops at any other function, such as a registered listener. A request (`fetch`, `axios`, `ky`, `XMLHttpRequest`) in that chain is reported by name. Otherwise a state setter (`set` plus a capital) in the chain that runs after an `await` of an enclosing level, inside a promise callback or passed to `.then` marks async work whose result reaches state; the earliest such step is reported once per effect at `Program:exit`. `react-no-effect-derived-state` picks its message from the setter's argument: a storage read (`localStorage`, `sessionStorage`) names `useSyncExternalStore`, a fixed value on a dependency list names a `key` reset, anything else the render-time computation. `react-no-random-key` follows one level of helper: a function in the same file whose returned expression calls a generator, or an exported function of a project module (`resolveModuleSpecifier()`, read with the TypeScript parser by `exportGenerates()` in `render/key-generators.mjs` and cached by file stamp). The eslint adapter drops a `no-restricted-properties` report at the same start as a `nitpicker/react-no-random-key` report (`supersedingRules` in `adapters/eslint/normalize.mjs`), since the native oxlint port cannot be wrapped the way `purity-outside-keys.mjs` wraps `react-x/purity`; `inKeyAttribute()` in `jsx.mjs` is the one key-attribute check both use. `oxlint-diagnostics.mjs` appends oxlint's `help` to the message of `no-restricted-properties` and `no-restricted-imports`, where oxlint puts the configured message. `purity-outside-keys.mjs` wraps `react-x/purity` with `withReportFilter()` and drops a report inside a `key` attribute, which `nitpicker/react-no-random-key` reports with the stable-id form. `static-components.mjs` wraps `react-x/static-components` so it reports a use only when the paired `createdHere` report points at a call or `new` (a nested function or class is left to `no-nested-component-definitions`) and drops the `createdHere` report itself. `accessibility-rules.mjs` holds the four accessibility rules added to the plugin's recommended set, `accessibilityHints` (the allowed form for each rule that has one, ending in `GENERAL_HINT`; other rules get `GENERAL_HINT` alone) and the options that differ from the plugin's defaults: `control-has-associated-label` ignores form fields (the nitpicker field rule covers them) and `label-has-associated-control` treats any capitalised component as a control. Analyzers that link `node_modules` into the snapshot (`eslint-*`, `typescript-typecheck`) declare `requiresDependencies`, so the run links it once and no analyzer removes it under another that is still running. `no-class-component` and `no-array-index-key` take their hints from `reactXHints` like every other `react-x` rule. TS2307, TS2792 and TS2882 for a stylesheet (`stylesheetExtensionNames` in `preset/layout.mjs`, shared with the module-load and styling rules), image or font specifier get an extra sentence naming `vite/client` and a `.d.ts` (`adapters/typecheck/asset-import-hint.mjs`); the finding identity is computed from the plain message.
- **Third-party rule messages.** `withHint()` in `rule-hints.mjs` wraps a plugin rule so its messages end with a nitpicker hint (the exception route, or the allowed form; an upstream message without closing punctuation gets a period first, unless the hint starts with its own separator as the Testing Library and i18n hints do), for rules that report by `messageId` and by `message`. The failure-message harness lints with the wrapped rules, so the hint is tested. Custom React rules (`react-no-effect-*`, `react-single-styling-approach`, `react-no-hardcoded-colors`) exist where no ESLint 10 plugin rule names the allowed form: `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect` is a React Compiler rule with a multi-paragraph message and no exception route.
- **i18n scope.** `eslint.config.mjs` adds `i18nScopeConfigFor()` (`eslint-config/i18n/i18n-scope.mjs`) only when `isI18nProject()` says so: the head policy's `i18n.enabled`, filled from the head checkout's manifest like `react.enabled` (see React scope; `react-intl` or `@formatjs/intl` in `dependencies` or `devDependencies`, read by `hasScopePackage()` in `eslint-config/scope-support.mjs`, which also holds `productionScope()` for both scopes). The head policy carries `i18n` (`enabled`, `functionNames`, `componentNames`; the last two become the plugin's `additionalFunctionNames` and `additionalComponentNames` settings). The plugin loads through a dynamic import, so a project without a message library never pays for it. `isI18nProject()` returns false for the JavaScript preset (`layout.javascript`) whatever the manifest or `i18n.enabled` says, and `validateConfig` rejects the `i18n` key with that preset, like `react`. The scope is one config entry for production source minus story files (`*.stories.*`, `*.story.*`, `storyGlobs` in `scope-support.mjs`, which the React scope also uses to turn `react-no-hardcoded-colors` off), independent of the React scope; its rules are syntactic, so they need no type information. `i18n-rules.mjs` is one table, `i18nRules`, of `{ setting, hint, messages?, filters?, ownsParseErrors? }` per enabled rule, plus `i18nOffRules`; `i18n-scope.test.mjs` checks that the two together name every rule of the installed plugin, so a rule a plugin upgrade adds fails the test until it is decided. `i18n-plugin.mjs` builds each rule from the upstream one with `withReportFilter()` (`rule-hints.mjs`, the one wrapper under `withHint()`, `withoutFixes()` and the i18n filters): `i18n-filters.mjs` drops a `no-literal-string-in-jsx` report whose text has no letter after entity decoding (a space, punctuation, a number, `&nbsp;`; the entities are decoded because oxlint hands over the raw JSX text), a `missingValue` report on a `FormattedMessage` whose `values` is not an object literal, a `placeholderAdjacentToWord` report for a message in a script written without spaces, and a `no-invalid-icu` report on a call with a literal `ignoreTag: true`; every rule except `no-invalid-icu` drops its `parseError` reports, because eight rules parse the same message and one syntax error would be reported eight times; and every rule reports at most once per node, because oxlint merges reports of one rule at one location while ESLint keeps both. Hints carry their own leading separator (`. ` or a space, chosen by how the rule's messages end), as the Testing Library hints do, and `messages` replaces the two upstream messages that embed newlines or say "function call or variable" for an empty string. Fixes are dropped: the text is for a person to write, and ESLint 10 runs a fixer at report time. Exceptions that name an i18n rule id (`i18nRuleIds`) are dropped before `applyRuleExceptions` while the scope is off; `validateConfig` rejects one that names a `formatjs/` rule nitpicker does not run. `i18nRuleIds` are strict rules (`strict-rules.mjs`). `eslint-plugin-formatjs` is listed in `ESLINT_PLUGIN_PACKAGES`, so its version is part of the `eslint-lint` analyzer version, and its scope files are in `checks/policy-files.mjs`. `i18n-engines.test.mjs` lints the same sources with ESLint and with oxlint and expects identical findings. The `eslint-config/` folder holds what both scopes share (`scope-support.mjs`, `rule-hints.mjs`); each scope has its own subfolder (`react/`, `i18n/`) because the folder holds at most six source and six test files.
- **Type-aware custom rules.** `nitpicker/exhaustive-union-predicate` sets `meta.docs.requiresTypeChecking`, so the oxlint engine hands it to the residual ESLint pass (`NITPICKER_DIFF_ESLINT_ONLY_RULES`) with every other type-aware rule, and it is registered in the typed entry of `strictTypedConfigFor()` beside the `@typescript-eslint` rules. It returns no listeners when the parser has no program. `nitpicker/no-forwarding-wrapper` is type-aware too: it compares the wrapper's and the callee's single call signature (parameter types and return type by identity, so a named alias or imported type counts and two structurally equal inline object types do not; the optional and rest flags of each parameter must match, and a signature with a type predicate, `asserts` included, on either side never matches) and finds the callee's binding through ESLint's scope analysis. `exhaustive-union-predicate` names the union from the alias of the value's declared type and decides "project or library" from where that alias, or an annotated declaration of the subject, is written, so a local binding that merely holds a library value is not mistaken for a project union. It reports only a chain in a value position (arrow body, `return`, variable initializer, assignment right side, looking through `!`, other logical operators and ternary branches), never a condition.
- **Component stories.** `structure/component-stories.mjs` is a project-level check run by the `structure` analyzer through `structure/project-findings.mjs`, next to the file limits. `syntax/exported-components.mjs` finds the exported real components of a file with the TypeScript parser (functions returning JSX, `memo` and `forwardRef` calls, classes extending `Component`; the parse kind follows the extension, and `.ts` files are not read). One walk of the source roots (`layout.projectCodeFiles`) collects both the components and the story files; story files (`storySuffixes` from `eslint-config/scope-support.mjs`, any code extension) are indexed by package directory and name, and a component file has a story when its stem (the folder for `index.*`) or any exported component name is in its package's index. Storybook presence is read per package, from the nearest `package.json` of the component. Both that and the more-than-half decision come from `liveProjectRoot`, the head checkout the runner is given on both snapshots, so a base run classifies exactly as the head run does: the comparison key includes the classification, so deciding it per side would turn every old finding into a new one when a pull request crosses the half mark. Below the half mark the findings are `descriptive` (listed, never failing); above it they are `enforceable`. Each component of a storyless file is one finding with the identity `component-story-missing:<Component>` (the file stem for an anonymous default export) and the component's line as its location. The rule id is in `RULE_IDS` and `RULES`; `RULE_TOOLS` lists it with the other rules nitpicker adds, so `applyRuleExceptions` accepts the id although no ESLint rule has it. The exception matcher and the copy-paste entry in the message come from `shared/rules/exceptions.mjs`, shared with the file limits. `structure/component-stories-cli.test.mjs` runs the check end to end through the CLI.
- **Prop-shape rules.** `eslint-plugin/rules/react-props/` holds the rules that read a component's props type. `react-components.mjs` finds components syntactically (a PascalCase function or `const` arrow, optionally inside `memo` or `forwardRef`, that returns JSX) and `syntax/component-names.mjs` owns the name test, which the structure analyzer's story check reuses. `nitpicker/no-runtime-exclusive-props-check` resolves the first parameter's destructured names (or `props.x`) and shares its `console` test with `no-production-console` (`consoleMethodOf`). `nitpicker/no-singular-and-plural-prop` expands unions, intersections and same-file aliases into branches (capped at 200) and pairs an optional `tag` with an optional `tags` array of the same type text inside one branch.
- **Type definitions.** `interface` is banned by a `no-restricted-syntax` selector, not `consistent-type-definitions`, because that rule cannot exempt `declare module` and `declare global` augmentations, which can only merge interfaces.
- **Failure messages.** Each case in `failure-messages/test-support/cases.mjs` has a `config`, a `banned` fixture that must fail, and an `allowed` wording (case-insensitive substring) the message must use to name the allowed form. Zod is a documented expectation of the preset, not a dependency.
- **Error wrapping.** Classified wrappers that sanitize external failures list `classificationIndex` and `unclassifiedValue`; only unclassified wrappers must carry the original cause.
- **Exceptions.** Lifted rules and paths come only from `nitpicker.config.json`; inline disables are banned, so this is the one way out. The base snapshot is measured under the base config's `exceptions` and `python.exceptions` cut down to what the head still has: `commonExceptions` in `core/policy-changes/base-coverage.mjs` (reached through `baseConfigCoveringHead`) keeps a base path only when a head entry with the same rule, `function` and `name` lists it. So removing or narrowing an entry in a change never turns the findings it lifted into new ones, and adding one accepts the finding the same change adds; the policy review reports the loosening itself. Python's function, file and size rules are entries of the same top-level `exceptions`; its ruff, type-checker and one-way rules stay in `python.exceptions`, which rejects the function rules. An entry whose paths match no file of the head tree is a non-failing note after the console summary (`core/exceptions/stale-exceptions.mjs`); the Java and Kotlin `stability-direction` entries are skipped, as their paths are packages.
- **Production policy.** `eslint-config/policy/production-policy.mjs` holds the production-wide rules that are plain ESLint options (`curly`, `no-eval`, `no-param-reassign`, `no-duplicate-imports`, naming convention, magic numbers, `regexp/no-super-linear-backtracking`) and wires the guard rules in `eslint-plugin/rules/guards/`. Naming and magic numbers run as `nitpicker/naming-convention` and `nitpicker/no-magic-numbers`: `eslint-plugin/rules/wrapped/` wraps the typescript-eslint rule and drops reports through `withReportFilter` (a `context` whose `report` is filtered), so the base rule's options and messages stay; a schema constant (also one derived from another schema constant, or from an imported PascalCase binding through a `SCHEMA_DERIVATIONS` method), a component-typed parameter, a number inside a module-level `const` initializer, an HTTP status in the status argument position of a response or error call (`STATUS_POSITIONS`) and a `toFixed` argument are the dropped reports. typescript-eslint marks `naming-convention` as needing type information, which would send it to the residual type-aware ESLint run under oxlint; the policy uses no `types` selector, so the wrapper clears that flag and, when the parser gives no TypeScript services (the oxlint bridge), hands the base rule empty node maps and no program. Route-handler names come from a native naming option instead: `defaults/eslint.config.mjs` passes `routeHandlers` when `usesRouteHandlerFramework` finds one of those frameworks in `package.json`, and `namingConvention` adds an exported-function selector filtered to the HTTP method names that accepts UPPER_CASE. `productionLayoutConfigFor()` holds `nitpicker/no-unknown-main-folder` apart: it reports on line 1 (the file's place, not its code), so it is not in `strictEslintRuleIds` and an edit to a legacy file does not fail; `defaults/eslint.config.mjs` adds it after the strict config, with `react: true` in a React project so the UI folders (`REACT_UI_FOLDERS`) and a feature's `ui/` pass. `strictPresetConfig` puts it first, so the scope entries after it can still replace an option. `no-restricted-properties` and `no-restricted-globals` replace like `no-restricted-syntax`: the `domain/` entry (`domainPurityRules`) comes after the production one and carries the full list. `no-process-access-outside-composition-root` is off for `layout.entryPoints` and config files; `no-module-level-mutable-state` skips `domain/`, which has its own readonly rules.
- **Heuristic guards.** Mutable module state, import-time effects and managed-resource construction match by name (`*Sync` calls, `*Client`/`*Pool` constructors, `create*` factories, `axios.create`), not by type, so a miss is possible and a false hit takes an `exceptions` entry. A try/catch in the composition root is deliberately not flagged. `no-unsafe-path-join` reads containment from the syntax, not from text: a `startsWith` whose prefix ends in a separator (`path-containment.mjs`), or `path.relative(...).startsWith('..')`, in an `if` before the call in an enclosing block whose branch exits, or around the call. `no-self-recursion` folds a callback into the enclosing function unless it goes to a timer or event registration (`DEFERRING_CALLEES`) or to a call that is a bare statement, which is how `emitter.on('change', () => watch())` stays allowed while `await limit(() => crawl(link))` is reported.
- **Boundary rule exceptions.** An `exceptions` entry may name a dependency-cruiser rule of `strictBoundaryRulesFor` (the `domain-*` rules); `exceptions.mjs` leaves those ids out of ESLint handling, and the architecture rules add the globbed `paths` to `from.pathNot`, so the paths are the importing files. `domain-does-not-reach-io-through-shared` uses `reachable: true`, which dependency-cruiser supports with `path` patterns only and reports with one depth-first path; `static-checks-runner.mjs` keeps a violation only when `sharedReachPath` finds a path over runtime (not type-only) edges whose first step is outside every feature and the composition root, and reports that path. A direct I/O import or an import of another domain file is left to the file's own rule. Via steps and targets are printed as dependency-cruiser resolved them, so a monorepo module such as `../libs/shared/clock.ts` appears with its `../`.
- **Unknown through aliases.** `no-unknown-in-domain` walks a same-file, non-generic type alias for any `unknown` (skipping type-parameter declarations and a `cause`/`error` slot) and follows the aliases it references. An alias imported from another file is resolved with the type checker, so the rule is marked `requiresTypeChecking` and runs in the residual type-aware ESLint run under oxlint, next to `no-mutating-method-in-domain` on the same `domain/` files; without parser services it checks same-file aliases only.
- **Analyzer cache keys.** `configured-analyzers.mjs` hashes each analyzer's adapter files and `configurationFiles` with `hashFiles`, which follows their relative imports (`shared/import-closure.mjs`), so a module a listed file imports is hashed without being listed; `POLICY_FILES` and `configurationFiles` only need the files a process loads by path. Each analyzer carries the list as `cacheKeyFiles`, and `configured-analyzers.test.mjs` checks that the import closure of every module its invocation starts (the runner, `--config`) is inside the hashed closure.
- **TypeScript-only compiler flags.** `TYPESCRIPT_ONLY_OPTIONS` (`exactOptionalPropertyTypes`, `noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature`, `noUncheckedSideEffectImports`) are added to the type-check tsconfig only; the mutation tsconfig keeps `POLICY_COMPILER_OPTIONS`, and JavaScript projects get the three set to false. `typecheck/compiler-hints.mjs` appends the allowed form to their errors (TS4111: bracket access; a message naming `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`: `field?: T | undefined`) in the finding's message only; the identity hashes the compiler's own text, so existing findings keep their identity.
- **Mutation with existing type errors.** Clean projects keep Stryker's TypeScript checker. When the project-derived mutation tsconfig still reports errors, `adapters/mutation-checker/` checks mutants against the unmutated snapshot's diagnostics instead. It uses the TypeScript compiler API with the project's compiler options and referenced projects; diagnostic counts are keyed by code, file, enclosing symbol and message, independent of line shifts. Existing errors cannot mark every mutant as a compile error: only additional diagnostics produce `CompileError`, and runnable mutants still reach the selected test runner. The separate differential `typescript-typecheck` analyzer continues to reject new source errors. This applies to TypeScript, checked JavaScript and React projects, and does not disable mutant compilation checks.
- **`strict-rules.mjs`.** The rule id list is written out because building `strictPresetConfig` loads typescript-eslint; `strict-rules.test.mjs` asserts it matches the real config.
- **Eslint runner.** Findings on comments (no-comments, ts-ignore, inline config) are anchored to the comment text, since the nearest syntax node would be the following code and editing it would change the fingerprint.
- **oxlint engine.** `oxlint/oxlint-plan.mjs` asks ESLint for each file's resolved config and groups files by rules, globals and `settings`; `oxlint-translation.mjs` turns each group into an oxlint override. The planning process also lists oxlint's native rules (`oxlint --rules --format json` with an explicit file argument, because without one oxlint walks its working directory and exits 1 with "No files found" when that folder is git-ignored, as nitpicker's own folder is inside `node_modules`) while ESLint loads the config, so the runner spawns no separate listing, and every oxlint subprocess runs with `NODE_COMPILE_CACHE` (a `nitpicker-node-compile-cache` folder in the temp dir, unless the caller set one), so the config modules that planning and the bridge both load are compiled once per Node version. oxlint reads `settings` only at the top of a config, so groups with different settings go into separate configs, one oxlint run each. `stage/` builds the temp stage and its directive rewrites, `oxlint-diagnostics.mjs` turns oxlint's JSON into ESLint results. Rules map to oxlint's ports, except `PARITY_BRIDGED_RULES` (ports that measured differently) and rules oxlint rejects the options of, which the runner retries as bridged. Bridged rules load from the ESLint plugin through `oxlint-bridge.mjs` under a `js-` alias, since oxlint reserves plugin names like `typescript-eslint`. Type-aware plugin rules oxlint cannot run go to a residual ESLint run limited by `NITPICKER_DIFF_ESLINT_ONLY_RULES`; a rule counts as type-aware when its `meta.docs.requiresTypeChecking` is true, so `react-plugins.mjs` sets it on the typed `react-x` rules (`typedReactXRules`) since they read `parserServices.program` (`oxlint-react.test.mjs` checks the routing). tsgolint ignores `--tsconfig` and uses the nearest `tsconfig.json`, so oxlint runs in a temp stage of symlinks whose `tsconfig.json` is the lint config (relative `extends` made absolute, `noEmit` on, and `baseUrl` replaced by `paths` rewritten to absolute targets, since tsgolint drops `baseUrl` and rejects `paths` that rely on it). Files under `noInlineConfig` get a stage copy with their directives hidden from oxlint by a U+FEFF before the keyword (same length, so offsets hold; `no-irregular-whitespace` findings at those spots are dropped), plus ESLint's "has no effect" warning. `oxlint-` directives are hidden the same way in every file, since ESLint would not honor them, and `eslint-` directives naming a bridged rule get its `js-` name. Nested `tsconfig.json` files on the way to a linted file are left out of the stage, so every file is checked against the lint config. tsgolint's `tsconfig-error` diagnostics for a missing `types` entry or an option TypeScript 7 dropped are discarded, since typescript-eslint tolerates them; any other config error fails the analyzer. Rule messages are matched against the ESLint rule's message templates to recover its `messageId`, so finding identities match the ESLint engine where the text matches. oxlint spans are UTF-8 bytes and are converted to UTF-16 columns. `eslint/no-unused-vars` stays native with never-matching ignore patterns, because oxlint's defaults differ from ESLint's and bridging it changes scope analysis.
- **ESLint project layer.** `resolveHeadPolicy` records `tools.eslint.projectConfigFile` (the first `ESLINT_PROJECT_FILES` entry at the head project root) and `tools.eslint.projectConfigDigest` when `precedence` lists `"project"`, or `tools.eslint.projectConfigMissing: true` without such a file; `withHeadCheckoutFacts` replaces all three keys of the base with the head's, so a stale `projectConfigMissing` cannot survive. The digest (`eslintConfigTree` in `preset/eslint-project/project-config.mjs`, path helpers in `config-paths.mjs`) is sha256 over the path and text of the config, the local files it imports (found with `ts.preProcessFile`, resolved with the module extensions, `.js` to `.ts` and `index` files, against the repository root, the nearest folder with `.git`, after `realpath`), the files a path-like string literal in them names (`ts.createSourceFile`), and `name@version` of each bare-specifier package (a workspace package, whose real folder is in the repository outside `node_modules`, adds its `package.json` and its `require.resolve` entry instead); anything resolving outside the repository is an error. `typescript` loads lazily through `createRequire`, so commands that only import `head-policy.mjs` (the shard aggregate) stay free of it. `lint-engine.mjs` hands every ESLint and oxlint process `NITPICKER_DIFF_ESLINT_PROJECT_LAYER`, a JSON object `{ config, nitpickerFirst, addsRules }` (`config` is that file under `--live-project-root`, the head checkout, for both snapshots; `addsRules` is true only for `LINT_CONFIG`), or an empty string; a policy with `projectConfigMissing` is the `missingProjectConfig` tool error, and a policy without either key (a base whose head did not opt in) gets no layer. `eslint-wrapper.config.mjs` passes the policy array through `layeredPolicy` (`lint-engine/project-layer.mjs`). `project-loader.mjs` imports the config (a `.ts`, `.mts` or `.cts` one through the first `jiti` with a `createJiti` export, resolved from the config's folder and then nitpicker's), normalizes it with `defineConfig` and rejects keys ESLint does not know;
