`basePath` is folded into the patterns. `project-rules.mjs` builds the owned set (rules some policy entry turns on, plugin names, settings keys) and, for `"project"` first, puts after each policy entry one entry per project entry that sets a rule it turns on, scoped to both entries (`files` as AND arrays, `ignores` joined), with the options merged by `mergedSetting` (list rules appended, `max-*` and `complexity` limits clamped, others replaced). The other project parts listed in usage.md follow the policy (or precede it for `nitpickerFirst`), and the entries named `RULE_EXCEPTION_ENTRY` move last, so an exception wins over the project; `applyRuleExceptions` with `projectRules` leaves unknown exception rules to `checkExceptionRules`, which the layer runs once the project's rules are in. The oxlint plan, the bridge and the residual ESLint run all load the same wrapper, so the layer reaches native rules through `calculateConfigForFile` and project plugins through `pluginRules`, and `NITPICKER_DIFF_ESLINT_ONLY_RULES` applies to project entries too. When a reviewed ESLint file changes and `"project"` is first, `readToolConfigs` runs `preset/eslint-project/report.mjs` once on the head (`replacedEslintOptions` in `lint-engine/replaced-options.mjs`), which loads the four ESLint check configs with the head policy and lists the rules `replacedOptionRules` finds; `toolConfigChanges` turns each into a loosening.
- **Test-code analysis.** `test-code-analysis.mjs` works on one file's syntax tree; calls into other modules are not followed. Per-test visitors report each node once however many tests reach it.
- **Runtime boundaries.** Roles select production modules by `modules` globs (relative to `src/main`) and list `forbiddenDependencies` and `forbiddenCalls` globs; the default declares none. Globstars do not traverse `..`, so relative spellings are resolved before matching.
- **Callback contracts.** Each entry names a `callee`, its `optionsArgument` index, `sourceCallbacks`, `correlatedCallbacks` (`{ name, inputIndex }`), `externalPublicationCallbacks` and `snapshotReadMethods`. Rules contain no consumer or file identities.
- **CRAP files.** `crap/source-files.mjs` lists the files to measure from the layout (`sourceFiles`: source roots, the layout extensions, generated and test directories left out) and `crap/report.mjs` reads their coverage; there is no directory expansion rule, every root is passed as the files inside it.
- **Test console output.** `test-console` is a quality-area analyzer that depends on `coverage` (like `crap`), runs no tests and takes the version of the project's test runner for its cache key, as `coverage` does; disabling `coverage` turns it off with `crap` (`applyDisable` turns off every transitive dependent of a disabled analyzer, `describeDisableCascade` words the stderr note), so a config written before it existed still applies. The test run adapters start the capture through `startTestConsole` (`adapters/test-runners/test-console-run.mjs`), which names the report in `NITPICKER_TEST_CONSOLE_REPORT` (`reports/quality/test-console/report.json` in the snapshot), the project root in `NITPICKER_TEST_CONSOLE_ROOT` and, for the runners whose test processes cannot write one file, a lines file in `NITPICKER_TEST_CONSOLE_LINES`; the capture is off in a `rerun` (the retiming of slow tests), so a partial run never replaces the report. That report is the whole contract with a test runner: `{ "entries": [{ "file", "test", "messages": ["..."] }] }`, one message per console call, `file` absolute (or empty for a Vitest log with no task) and `test` possibly empty. Each runner has one producer loaded by path inside the user's process, and they share `console-report.mjs` (normalization to messages, placeholders, the report writer, a block's group indent removed first so a call inside `console.group` reads like one outside it, whichever runner indented it). The ESM producers are `*.config.mjs` files in `preset/test-console/` (what the lint policy lets read `process.env` and default-export), and each only passes `process.env` (and, for Jest, the working directory; for Mocha, `process.argv[1]`) to a function of a sibling module that holds the logic and is tested directly. A producer does not normalize: it hands over the split messages, and the shared `testConsoleFindings` (`shared/rules/console/`, the `test-console-output` fact rule that `FACT_RULE_LANGUAGES` registers for TypeScript and JavaScript alike) normalizes them with `normalizeConsoleMessage` and applies `testConsole.allow` through `consoleAllowMatcher`, so no runner has its own copy. Vitest: `vitest-reporter.config.mjs`, a second `--reporter` of the coverage run. Jest: `runners/jest-reporter.config.mjs` (logic in `jest-reporter.mjs`), passed as `--reporters=default --reporters=<path>` with `--silent=false --verbose=false`, because only `BufferedConsole` keeps `testResult.console` (a `silent` project gets a `NullConsole` and a `verbose` one, or a run of one file, which Jest makes verbose, a `CustomConsole` that prints at once; only reporters see the buffer: Jest drops it from the aggregated results, so a `testResultsProcessor` gets none). It keeps the `error`, `warn` and `assert` types (`console.trace` is an `error` entry) per test file and rewrites Jest's `AssertionError [ERR_ASSERTION]: <first argument>` as Node's `Assertion failed: ...` (Jest drops the other arguments); Jest gives no timing for those entries, so there is no test name. After a file's tests Jest freezes its console: a later call prints "Cannot log after tests are done" and sets `process.exitCode = 1`, which fails a run in band (one file, or `-i`). With the forced `BufferedConsole` that also happens to a `silent` or `verbose` project (`CustomConsole` prints errors and warnings through `_logError`, which the freeze does not replace), so `runJest` treats an exit of 1 as a pass when Jest's report passed everything (`success`, no failed test or suite, no runtime error, no snapshot `failure`, not interrupted), standard error holds that text, and `--showConfig` shows `silent` or `verbose` (`shownConfig`, global or any project) or the run had one test file; a buffered multi-file run in band still fails, as it does without nitpicker. The late call is not recorded, as with Vitest, whose worker ends with the file. `node:test`: the coverage preload `v8-coverage/loaded-sources.cjs`, which the coverage run passes with `--require` and in `NODE_OPTIONS`, so every process of the run loads it, including those a test spawns. It records only in a test file process the runner started: the runner process (no `NODE_TEST_CONTEXT`, `--test` in `execArgv`) writes its pid to `NITPICKER_TEST_CONSOLE_RUNNER` when that variable is empty (`startTestConsole` sets it to `''` for the run, so an inner `node --test` that a test starts with `NODE_TEST_CONTEXT` removed inherits the outer pid, claims nothing, and its files do not record), and a process records when `NODE_TEST_CONTEXT` starts with `child`, `argv[1]` is set and its parent is that pid; a process a test starts has the test file process as its parent, so it never loads `node:test` and keeps its own output and exit code. There it takes the file from `argv[1]` and registers root hooks that keep the stack of running test names (`fullName`): `beforeEach` pushes the name, and `afterEach` (which runs before the project's root `afterEach` hooks) adds a `t.after` hook that pops it, which node:test runs after every `afterEach` hook and after the test's own `t.after` hooks, so those keep the test name. Its root `after` hook, which runs first, adds one more root `after` hook through its context (`context.after`), which node:test appends and runs last because it rereads the hook list; that one stops recording one `setImmediate` later, so the project's root `after` hooks count, async ones too, and a timer that fires later does not. A context without `after` stops at once. With `--experimental-test-isolation=none` the runner process runs the tests and nothing records (root hooks the preload registers there never run for the files' tests, so the file of a call is unknown). The preload has to be a CommonJS file with no ESM in it: `--import` loads the main file through the ESM loader and skips `require.extensions` hooks such as `ts-node/register`, and a `require` of an ESM file breaks `mock.module` (`resolveSync() is not implemented`) and `tsx`; it exports `recordConsole`, `LINES_VARIABLE` and `MAX_CONTENT_LENGTH` for the ESM modules, so the variable name and the 4000-character cut exist once. Mocha: `runners/mocha-capture.config.mjs` (logic in `mocha-capture.mjs`), passed as `--require`, a root hook plugin whose `mochaHooks` function adds no hooks: it resolves the running Mocha from `process.argv[1]` (the Mocha CLI or its parallel worker script), wraps `Mocha.prototype.loadFiles`/`loadFilesAsync` to listen to the root suite's `pre-require`/`post-require` (the loading file) and hook-added events (the file of a root-level hook), and `Runner.prototype.run` to listen to `hook`, `test` and `end`. Each of those sets the current place (a `beforeEach`/`afterEach` hook takes its test, a `before`/`after` hook its suite's file and title, a test its `file` and its `titlePath()` joined as `suite > test`) and enters it into an `AsyncLocalStorage`, so a timer or promise keeps the place where it started; a call whose origin is another spec file (a timer from a finished file) is not recorded. A call whose origin has no file (work that a root hook from a `--require` file or `mochaGlobalSetup` started, such as a server) takes the current place, so a request handler that logs during a test belongs to that test, as with Vitest; a call made in such a root hook itself, after `end` or with no file at all (Mocha's own warnings in the main process) is not recorded. The place lives in the process, never in `process.env`, so a process a test spawns inherits nothing and, since Mocha's `--require` is not a Node option, does not load it. It works in `--parallel` workers too, which load the `--require` modules themselves. Both wrap only `console.error` and `console.warn`: Node's `console.trace` and `console.assert` print through `this.error` and `this.warn`, so each call is recorded once with Node's own `Trace: ` and `Assertion failed: ` text. The wrapper formats the call as Node would (`util.format`), cuts it to 4000 characters and appends one raw JSON line `{ file, test, content }` to the lines file, with `openSync`/`fstatSync`/`writeSync` taken from `fs` and the file opened when the preload loads, so a test that replaces `fs.writeFileSync` (`mock.method`, sinon) never sees the write. mock-fs replaces the fs binding and numbers its own descriptors from 3, so the writer first checks that `fstatSync` of its descriptor gives the device and inode the file had when it was opened; when it does not (or fails) the line waits and is retried on the next call, so a line never lands in a mocked file. At `exit`, lines still waiting (a mock left active) are appended by a `node -e` child started with `spawnSync`, whose `fs` is not mocked. Nothing the wrapper does throws into the console call. It then calls the original, so a project that stubs the method later also hides the call from the check, as with a silenced Vitest log. The parent normalizes: `startTestConsole(...).finish()` skips a line that is not JSON with a string `content` (a process killed mid-write), runs each line through `consoleEntry` (placeholders for the project root, temporary and home directories, then the splitting into messages) and writes the report right after the run, an empty one if nothing was printed. The Vitest reporter keeps `onUserConsoleLog` entries of type `stderr`, which is where Node's console sends `console.error`, `console.warn`, `console.trace` and a failed `console.assert`. It looks the entity up with `state.getReportedEntityById` (Vitest 4 and 5), skips a log the project's `onConsoleLog` returns `false` for (the call the default reporter makes), and replaces the project root, `os.tmpdir()` and `os.homedir()` (and their real paths) by `<root>`, `<tmp>` and `<home>` so the two snapshots' temporary directories agree. Vitest batches the console calls of one task made in the same microtask into one log, and its `size` is the number of calls: a log is cut into blocks at each unindented line that is not a closing bracket and each block is one message, whether the log holds one call or several (`log.size` is not read), so a call gives the same message alone or batched; the same splitting applies to every runner, and a message is the lines of its call that have a letter or digit, without indented `at` and `in` lines (stack frames, component stacks), joined by spaces. `console-messages.mjs` (in `shared/rules/console/`, so every preset's console findings use it) normalizes a message (terminal colors written as the escape character, as `?` (Gradle) or as `&#27;`/`&amp;#27;` (Surefire); control characters and lone surrogates, whether raw, written as an entity or replaced by `?` between two letters or digits, are dropped so a Gradle and a Maven report agree; the thread name of a Spring Boot or Logback line as `[<thread>]`; paths to their last segment, ISO timestamps, `java.util.logging` clock lines (`Oct 17, 2026 3:04:05 AM`) and clock times, ISO-8601 durations (`PT1M12.345S`) and compound ones (`1h2m3s`) as `<duration>`, the random part of Mockito (`$MockitoMock$`) and ByteBuddy class names and the number of `$$SpringCGLIB$$0`-style proxies, a trailing counter on a word of three or more lowercase letters (`conn6`), `0x` addresses, UUIDs, mixed letter-and-digit tokens of six or more characters, temporary names (`tmp` and six or more of `[a-z0-9_]`, `tmp.` or `tmp-` and six or more letters or digits, and a name under the temporary directory that ends in a separator and six random characters; such a name has no digit to recognize, which is why the temporary directory is part of the rule), every number with its unit, spaces; 300 characters) and `console-findings.mjs` makes one finding per test file and message: identity `console:<hash of the message>`, so the test name, the order and the repeat count are not part of it. A message of three or more words also carries `tokens` (its words), the optional finding field that `compareFindings` uses in `matchSimilarFindings` (`core/diff/comparison/fingerprint.mjs`): a head finding with no exact match counts as present when the base has an unmatched finding with the same tool, rule, path (renames applied) and classification, the same number of tokens and at most one differing token, and that token pair passes `isOpaqueChange` (`core/diff/comparison/similar/opaque-token.mjs`: both tokens, without edge punctuation, are lowercase letters and digits of five or more characters, or carry an `<id>` placeholder, and at least one has a run of four consonants, a vowel ratio under 0.2 or letters mixed with digits; capitalized, camelCase, UPPERCASE and backticked tokens never qualify). Pairs are chosen as a maximum matching (augmenting paths, heads and base findings in identity order), found through an index keyed by the tokens with one position left out and the class of the token left out (random-looking, plain, `<id>` or `<n>`), so only findings that can be paired are ever compared. The search for a longer path stops after two million neighbor visits and keeps the pairs found, which only matters for thousands of messages that differ in one random word. This keeps random values that no pattern can recognize from making unchanged output new without hiding a changed ordinary word. `testConsole.allow` entries (normalized `contains`, optional `paths`) and `exceptions` for `test-console-output` come from the head policy file, so base and head use the same ones. Without a report the runner returns a `test-console-report-missing` tool error; when the tests fail `coverage` fails first and the scheduler skips this analyzer.
- **TypeScript API selection.** TypeScript 7's package exports only `lib/version.cjs` and its `unstable/*` APIs, with no `lib/typescript.js`. nitpicker's own modules import TypeScript through `typescript-api/typescript-api.mjs`, which loads the `typescript` that resolves from nitpicker's location when it has a `lib/typescript.js` next to its main file, and otherwise the `typescript-js-api` npm alias (TypeScript 6.0, the JavaScript-API release of TypeScript 7's behavior). In that case the CLI entry (`core/cli.mjs`, through `redirectToBundledTypeScriptApi`) registers a synchronous `module.registerHooks` resolve hook (`api-redirect.mjs`) before it runs. Whenever a module inside a `node_modules` package imports `typescript` and it would resolve to a package without `lib/typescript.js`, or not at all, the hook sends `typescript`, `typescript/package.json` and `typescript/lib/*` (such as `tsserverlibrary`) to the alias, so typescript-eslint, Stryker's TypeScript checker, dependency-cruiser and the oxlint JS plugins load the same API. dependency-cruiser needs the manifest redirect: it reads `typescript/package.json` and silently stops parsing TypeScript when the version is 7 or later, which leaves the module graph empty. The hook also appends a `--import=data:` preload that registers the same hook to `NODE_OPTIONS`, so every Node process nitpicker starts inherits it. `typescript/unstable/*` is left alone, and so is an import from outside `node_modules`, so the project's own sources and tests, which nitpicker's test runs also start with that `NODE_OPTIONS`, keep the project's TypeScript 7. The minimum is Node.js 22.19 (`supportsRedirect`): `registerHooks` exists from 22.15, but with an `--import` preload, Node versions before 22.19 load a CommonJS main through the ESM loader and skip `require.extensions` hooks such as `ts-node/register` (the reason the coverage preload below uses `--require`); from 22.19 they work. On older Node a TypeScript 7 project fails at startup with exit code 2 and that requirement. The analyzer versions of TypeScript consumers (ESLint, the structure and CRAP analyzers, the dependency-cruiser analyzers and the type check) record `js-api-<version>` when the alias is in use.
- **Type check engine.** `typecheck/typescript-program.mjs` returns the pre-emit diagnostics. The `native` engine (`native-diagnostics.mjs`) loads TypeScript 7's `unstable/sync` API from the `typescript-native` npm alias, or its `unstable/async` API under Deno and Bun (`apiEntry`): the sync API reads and writes `tsgo`'s stdio pipes through Node.js's internal file descriptors, which those runtimes do not have, while the async one talks JSON-RPC over the pipe streams. Both answer the same requests, so the engine awaits each one and the findings are the same. It sends `initialize` before anything else, so a `tsgo` that cannot answer counts as an API that cannot start. It opens the config as a project and collects config, program, syntactic, global, bind and semantic diagnostics (declaration ones when `declaration` or `composite` is set), the set `getPreEmitDiagnostics` returns. The CLI is not used because it stops after the first stage with errors, so a missing `types` entry would hide every semantic error. Diagnostics are reshaped into TypeScript 6 objects (a `createSourceFile` per file read with `ts.sys.readFile`, so BOMs and UTF-16 are handled as the project engine does, with `path` set for sorting; `messageChain` turned into a `next` chain; program diagnostics located in a `.json` config made file-less, because TypeScript 6 reports option errors without a location) and go through `sortAndDeduplicateDiagnostics`, so messages, symbols, order and identities match the `project` engine. Any config parsing error, a removed-option diagnostic (TS5102, TS5108) or an API that cannot start (no platform binary) makes the run fall back to the project's TypeScript. TypeScript itself is passed in as `ts` from `loadTypeScript`: the project's `lib/typescript.js`, or the bundled API above when the project's `typescript` is TypeScript 7. A project without `typescript` (`isToolInstalled`) gets the bundled API with the project engine; on a TypeScript 7 project `projectTypeScript` also returns its package root as `native`, and the `project` engine (and the `native` engine's fallback) opens the project's own `unstable/sync` (or `unstable/async`) API there instead of `typescript-native`, with no further fallback: config errors fail the analyzer and removed-option diagnostics are reported as findings. On every engine an unknown option (TS5023, TS5025, `NON_FATAL_CONFIG_CODES`) is reported as a finding and the type check runs, as `tsc` does: the project engine passes the parse errors to `createProgram` as `configFileParsingDiagnostics`, and the project's TypeScript 7 sorts them in with the program's diagnostics through source files without a `path`, so they come first and are located in the config that sets the option unless that is the opened (generated) config, as TypeScript 6 does. When the config matches no files, only those config diagnostics are reported. Other config errors are thrown marked `configError`, and `preEmitDiagnostics` prefixes them with `configName`. `static-checks/typescript.mjs` reads the `extends` of the generated config to find the project's config and uses it as the path of file-less diagnostics and of diagnostics located in the generated config or a config outside the project, falling back to nitpicker's policy config when the project has none. Base and head are both checked with the TypeScript that `liveProjectRoot` resolves. `resolveTool` checks an `npm:` alias against the aliased package's name and version. The analyzer version lists both TypeScript versions, plus the bundled API's version when it is in use. `withoutProjectRoot` makes absolute paths in messages relative to the snapshot root, which differs between base and head and between runs.
- **Dependency cruiser.** The policy TypeScript config is linked into the analyzed root so its include globs resolve against analyzed sources; `typeScriptRoot` defaults to this package and is overridden for fixtures. `dependency-graph/dependency-cruiser.config.mjs` reads the policy tsconfig path from `NITPICKER_DEPENDENCY_GRAPH_TSCONFIG` when it is first imported; the in-process test fixture sets a placeholder for that import and passes the linked tsconfig on each call.
- **Reuse checks.** `shared-used-by-one-feature` runs in the `coupling` runner (`coupling/shared-usage.mjs`, `coupling/evaluate-coupling.mjs`: the feature and shared patterns are `architecture/strict-layout.mjs`, and the runner hands the head policy's exceptions in); the other five run in the `structure` runner through `structureProjectFindings()` (`structure/reuse/`): `structure/reuse/reuse-findings.mjs` turns the parsed files into facts (`definition-facts.mjs`) and hands `duplicate-definition`, `exported-name-collision` and `sibling-copy-edit` to `sharedFactFindings` (see [Facts schema](#facts-schema-sharedrulesfacts)), and runs the two React rules itself; `sibling-copy-edit` also reads the base checkout the head snapshot gets as `baselineRoot`, and reports nothing in the base snapshot or a run without one. It decides which copies were edited by comparing each head file with the base file at its old path: the diff engine writes the renames it already detected (`layout.moves.files`) to a temporary file (`git/patches/moves-file.mjs`) and hands its path to the head run of each quality-area analyzer as `--baseline-moves-file`, the structure runner passes them on as `baselinePaths` (`quality-areas/base-paths.mjs`), and a file whose text equals its base file is never parsed. They read each tree's own `package.json` and `tsconfig.json` (imports resolve through the shared resolver in `modules/module-resolution.mjs`; a workspace package linked into `node_modules` resolves to its source, and `structure/reuse/reuse-paths.mjs` maps a path in the live checkout that a snapshot's `node_modules` links to back into the snapshot, for the structure checks and the coupling graph alike), except that the React scope and the icon package are decided once from the head checkout (`react.enabled`, `react.iconPackage` in the head policy). Their results depend on files the runners import, so `coupling/check-files.mjs` and `structure/check-files.mjs` list the entry modules of each check: the cache key hashes their import closure, and a new module is covered as long as an existing entry imports it. `layout.mjs` holds the one generated-path test (`isGeneratedPath`: the built-in output folders at the project root, `node_modules` and `__generated__/` at any depth, `*.generated.*`, `*.gen.*`, `*.pb.*`, `*_pb.*` files) that source selection, the project code scan, the reuse checks and the svg scan share; a source folder named like build output is compared like any other, and generated code elsewhere belongs in `exclude`.
- **One import graph per side.** `dependency-architecture`, `coupling`, `test-runtime` and `entry-points` all read `loadDependencyGraph` (`adapters/dependency-graph/project-dependency-graph.mjs`): one dependency-cruiser run over the existing graph roots with the architecture config, shared through the build directory above. Architecture reads `summary.violations`; the others read `modules`. Coupling and entry-points restrict themselves to their own roots (`internalEdges`, `isSourceUnderRoot`, `startsWith(root/)`), so the test roots in the graph do not change their results; test-runtime uses every module, and its roots are the same as before, so its input is unchanged. The key covers project, policy files, roots, exclusion, `--include-only` and the cruiser binary; a request that differs in any of them cruises on its own. Entry points whose source root is not a graph root cruise that root directly.
- **Dependency hooks.** A hook has `language`, `manifests` (the first marks a project; if any differs from the live policy root, compared byte for byte, the snapshot needs its own install instead of a link), `directory`, `dependencyRoot(projectRoot)` and `install(projectRoot)`, which installs a regular directory and cleans up and throws on failure. The one hook (`dependency-hooks.mjs`) serves TypeScript and JavaScript and is package-manager aware (`dependency-graph/package-managers/`). `managerRoot()` (`detect.mjs`) is the nearest folder from the project up to the repository root (the first folder with a `.git`; without one the project folder alone) that holds a lockfile, and is where `node_modules` lives, where the install runs and where `manifests` are compared (a different relative position of that folder on the two sides counts as a difference). `manifests` lists `package.json`, every lockfile, `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, `.npmrc`, `.yarnrc`, `.yarnrc.yml` and `bunfig.toml`. `detectPackageManager()` reads the `packageManager` field of the root `package.json` (parsed; the text of a malformed file is matched instead; a known name wins), then the first lockfile in the order pnpm, yarn, bun, npm, else npm, and gives Yarn a `major`: the field's, else the `__metadata` version of `yarn.lock` (4 and below is Yarn 2), else the `yarn lockfile v1` header (1), else `.yarnrc.yml` (Yarn 3+). `installInvocation()` (`commands.mjs`) holds the frozen command per manager and Yarn major (Yarn 2 `--skip-builds`, 3 and later `--mode=skip-build`, which also stops the root workspace's scripts), runs it through `env` with `COREPACK_ENABLE_AUTO_PIN=0` (a Corepack shim must not write `packageManager` into the base) and, for Yarn, `YARN_IGNORE_PATH=1`, because a module outside the composition root may not read `process.env` to merge variables; pnpm gets `--ignore-pnpmfile`. These keep repository code from running during the install; Yarn plugins cannot be neutralized, so `install.mjs` refuses a Yarn 2+ base whose `.yarnrc.yml` has `plugins`. The same file maps a missing manager (exit status 127 of `env`), a timeout, an output overflow and a signal to messages, refuses native Windows, and builds the messages for a live project without `node_modules` (Plug'n'Play only for Yarn 2+) and for an install that left none (an empty folder is made for the other managers, a project may have no dependencies). `members.mjs` lists the workspace members' `node_modules` folders from `git ls-files` (tracked and untracked members, no crawl of the tree); `install()` returns `memberPaths`, those that did not exist before the install, and removes them with the root on failure. Link mode (`member-links.mjs`) builds each member `node_modules` as a real folder of symlinks, scoped folders included, and points an entry that resolves to a workspace package at the snapshot's copy of it, so the base never reads the head's sibling source; `removeSnapshotDependencies()` removes members first and the root even when a member removal throws. The root `node_modules` is still one link to the live folder, so a hoisted layout (npm, Yarn or Bun workspaces) resolves siblings through the live tree. Tests: `package-managers/test-support/fake-managers.mjs` puts fake `npm`, `pnpm`, `yarn` and `bun` programs (`fixtures/fake-package-manager.sh`, which records its arguments and the two variables) first on `PATH`; `real-package-managers.test.mjs` runs each real manager on the lockfile fixtures in `fixtures/projects/` (versions pinned by their `packageManager` fields), proves with marker files that a `.pnpmfile.cjs`, `yarn-path` and `yarnPath` do not run, and skips a manager that is not installed unless `CI` is set.
- **Unused props, options and exports.**
  - Program: `structure` builds one TypeScript program (`unused-api/analyze.mjs`) from the layered policy `tsconfig` that the type check uses (so `extends`, `paths` and project references resolve the same way, parsed with `allowJs`), plus root `*.config.*` files, `scripts/`, and every code file of the other workspace packages, whose names `workspace/entries.mjs` maps through `paths` to the source entry their manifest names (`source`, `types`, `main`, `exports`) or to a source root's `index`, and `name/*` into the source roots. The program sets `types: []` so no `@types` package is parsed, and it is not built at all when a syntax-only pass over the source files finds no component with props and no options parameter.
  - Targets: registered by declaration node (function declarations, `const` functions, the function inside `memo` or `forwardRef`, and `const Y = memo(X)` or `export default memo(X)` aliases); members whose props or options type another declaration also uses (`syntax/shared-types.mjs`) are dropped, as are parameters typed by a type parameter.
  - References: one pass over every file (`references/`) resolves JSX tags, call callees, `createElement`, `jsx` and `cloneElement` calls and `X.defaultProps` assignments through imports, aliases and re-exports with the checker (`getSymbolAtLocation` plus `getAliasedSymbol`) and records, per target, which props or options each reference passes and from which kind of file (production or support: the layout's test files and fixtures, stories, `.storybook` and `test-support`). `cloneElement` props count for every component. An identifier that refers to a target in any other position, and every export of a module loaded by `import()`, `require()` or `import.meta.glob` (a template-literal specifier loads every module under its static prefix) or used as a namespace value, is an escape and ends the judging of that target. A re-export statement is not a reference. Members count only when a declaration lies in an owned file: a production TypeScript file of the project that is not generated. Identity is `<name>.<member>` (`#n` for repeats), so no line number or file moves it.
  - Test-only exports: `dead-code` runs knip a second time with the config of `test-only/production-config.mjs`: the normal config with test, `__tests__` and story globs negated in `project`, test entries dropped and the test-runner plugins turned off, so scripts and plugin configs stay entries (knip's `--production` would drop them). The second run's `exports`, `types` and `files` that the normal run does not list are `test-only-export` (`files` under `test-support/` excepted).
  - Re-exports: knip counts a re-export from an entry as a use. `test-only/reexported-exports.mjs` reads the re-exports of the entry files (knip's `entry` globs, `layout.entryPoints` and the files the manifests name, found with the same skip rules as the walk of the whole project: no dot, excluded or linked directory, no declaration file) from the syntax (`syntax/module-facts.mjs`, specifiers resolved with `modules/module-resolution.mjs`; `test-only/reexport-graph.mjs` follows named, renamed, `export *` and `export * as` re-exports, imports that are only exported again and `export const y = x` aliases of an import that the file does not read, together with the further aliases of it (`export default y`, `export { y as z }`, `export const w = y`), through any number of files, to the declarations; a namespace that production reads as a whole value uses every member, and one read as `ns.member` only that member). Only when a test reads such a declaration does it run knip a third time, the second config with `includeEntryExports`. A declaration is `test-only-export` when that run lists it as unused (or, for an import that is only exported again, which hides it from knip, when no production file, script or tool config references it), when the entry's own export is listed too (knip leaves out what a framework plugin consumes, such as a Next route, and never lists the name of an `export * as`, which therefore counts as unconsumed), when it is in the same package as the entry and inside the project, and when it is not on the public surface of a published package or of a `layout.entryPoints` file. A declaration that nothing imports, tests included, is not reported. A finding that knip does not list has the line of its declaration. Limits: code that reads an import without being reachable (an unused local alias, a function nobody calls) still counts as a use, as it does for knip; an entry that spreads a namespace (`{ ...feat }`, `export default feat`) uses it, and a host that loads an entry without importing it has to be listed in `layout.entryPoints`.
  - Published packages: a package counts as published when its `package.json` has a name, is not `"private": true` and has one of `main`, `exports`, `bin`, `files` or `publishConfig`; `workspace/packages.mjs` finds workspace members from `workspaces` or the `packages:` key of `pnpm-workspace.yaml` (with `!` negations), looking for the workspace root no higher than the repository root.
  - Failures: the runners (`runners/unused-api.mjs`, `runners/program/design-signals.mjs`, both through `runners/program/program-analysis.mjs`) turn a failure into a tool-error finding, so the analyzer's other findings still report.
  - Project knip config: with `tools.knip.precedence` listing `"project"` and `dead-code` not disabled, `withProjectToolConfigs` (`preset/project-tools/project-tool-policy.mjs`) captures the config into the head policy as `projectConfig` and `projectConfigDigest`, or `projectConfigError` when it is missing, escapes the project after `realpath` (`realFileInside` in `shared/conventions/read-inside.mjs`, shared with jscpd) or does not load. A JSON or JSONC file and the `package.json` key are parsed in process with `parseJsonc` (`shared/jsonc/jsonc.mjs`, the same comment and trailing-comma rules as knip's `parseJSONC`). A script file is evaluated in a child process: `node --input-type=module -e` with a bootstrap that imports `config-loader.mjs` and calls `writeToolConfig`, cwd the checkout, `withoutHostCiOutputs` (`core/tooling/host-environment.mjs`, the filter analyzer processes get) as the environment, stdout ignored, a 60-second `timeout` with `SIGKILL`; the result (`{ config }` or `{ failure }`) comes back in a JSON file in a temporary folder, so a config that prints, exits early or hangs gives a clear failure. The child loads it with the pinned knip's own `loadResolvedConfigFile` (`dist/util/load-config.js`); `compilers`, `syncCompilers` and `asyncCompilers` are dropped at the top level and in each workspace (`withoutKnipCompilers`), a regular expression is stored as `{ "$nitpickerRegExp": [source, flags] }` (`config-values.mjs`) and any other value that is not plain data fails the capture with its key path. The capture runs in the live checkout, before the snapshots exist, because the head policy and its cache key are resolved first. `adapters/quality-areas/knip/knip-layers.mjs` merges it with the layout config (`layeredKnipConfig`); a nested `workspaces` map gets a `"."` entry with the root's workspace keys and plugins when written (`writtenKnipConfig`), because knip reads only that entry for the root once `workspaces` exists. `knip-run.mjs` writes `.nitpicker-diff-knip.json`, or `.mjs` with a reviver when a regular expression is present, and starts knip with an `--import` data URL that calls `guardKnipManifest` (`manifest-guard.mjs`) with the snapshot's `package.json`: the guard wraps `fs.promises.readFile` (`syncBuiltinESMExports`) and, for a read whose `realpath` is that file, drops the `knip` key (parsing JSONC; a file that does not parse but names `knip` makes the read throw), because knip applies `Object.assign({}, manifest.knip, config)` even under `--config` and reads `package.json` from its real working directory. A recorded `projectConfigError` is thrown when `dead-code` runs, so the analyzer fails as a tool error. For policy changes, `appliedProjectConfigs` (`project-tools/changes/project-config-changes.mjs`) evaluates the config on the head side and, when the base opts in, the base side (`{}` otherwise) with `evaluatedProjectConfig`, and `knipConfigChanges` compares the applied value of each key (canonical JSON, list keys as sorted sets, only negated `project` patterns, ranked keys only when the head's precedence starts with `"project"`); when a side fails to load, `toolConfigChanges` falls back to the file fingerprints. Without the opt-in, `scriptProjectToolStyle` (`ignored-notices.mjs`, the convention style of the TypeScript and JavaScript presets) adds one `ignoredConfigNotice` line per config it finds to the conventions summary.
  - Project dependency-cruiser config: captured the same way while one of `dependency-architecture`, `coupling`, `test-runtime` and `entry-points` is enabled. A `.dependency-cruiser.json` without `extends` is parsed in process (one that does not parse as JSON, such as JSON5, goes to the child); any other file is evaluated in the child by the pinned dependency-cruiser's `extractDepcruiseConfig` (`src/config-utl/extract-depcruise-config/index.mjs`, which follows `extends`) through `loadDependencyCruiserConfig` (`project-tools/dependency-cruiser-loader.mjs`): before loading, it registers a `module.registerHooks` resolve hook that rejects a local specifier (relative, absolute or `file:`) from a project file outside `node_modules` whose target's `realpath` is outside the checkout, and walks the local `extends` chain with dependency-cruiser's `read-config.mjs`, rejecting a target outside the checkout (`isRealPathInside`). `withDependencyCruiserFiles` (`config-values.mjs`) resolves `webpackConfig.fileName` and `babelConfig.fileName` against the head checkout on both paths. `loadDependencyGraph` (`adapters/dependency-graph/project-dependency-graph.mjs`) reads the layer with `projectGraphLayer` (`project-config/graph-layer.mjs`): a recorded `projectConfigError` is thrown only when the caller passes `reportsConfigErrors` (the `dependency-architecture` run in `static-checks-runner.mjs`), so the coupling, test-runtime and entry-points checks read the graph without the layer. It adds the project's `options.exclude` paths to the `--exclude` pattern (the CLI flag replaces the config's `exclude`; a regular expression becomes its `source`, `patternSource`) and the layer to the graph key, and writes a wrapper config that calls `layeredDependencyCruiserConfig` (`project-config/dependency-cruiser-layers.mjs`) on the policy config inside the dependency-cruiser process, because the policy config reads the head policy when it loads. Named rules merge like dependency-cruiser's own `extends` (the higher layer's properties over the lower's), unnamed and `allowed` rules are deduplicated by value, `DEPENDENCY_CRUISER_OWNED_OPTIONS` (`config-values.mjs`; a test checks it covers every option of the policy config and every flag `cruiseArguments` passes) keep the policy's value, `doNotFollow` paths (as sources) and dependency types are unioned and `enhancedResolveOptions.extensions` stays the policy's. For policy changes, `dependencyCruiserConfigChanges` (`project-tools/changes/dependency-cruiser-config-changes.mjs`) flattens `options` into `options.<key>`, skips the owned options, compares `exclude` and `doNotFollow` as sets of `path <source>` and `dependency type <type>` entries and the rule lists as sets, and counts added paths, added `allowed` rules, dropped or changed rules, added named rules and any other changed key as loosenings.
- **Design facts.** `structure` also runs `runners/program/design-signals.mjs` after the unused-API analysis: `preset/design/analyze.mjs` builds its own program with `createProjectProgram` and `createContext` (`withoutTests`: test files and fixtures are not root names, so the program is smaller and nothing else in the program can reach a test) and hands `designFacts` (`preset/design/facts.mjs`) to `sharedFactFindings` with `DESIGN_RULES`, the language (`javascript` when `layout.javascript`) and the head policy plus its `protocols`. The program is not shared with the unused-API pass, which needs the test files to tell production callers from support callers. Modules are the owned source files (inside the project, production role, not generated by the policy or a `@generated` header), `entry` comes from `layout.entryPoints`. `published` (`preset/design/publication.mjs`) follows the public surface `test-only-export` uses: the files that `layout.entryPoints` and the manifest entries (`source`, `types`, `typings`, `module`, `main`, `exports`; not `bin`) name, kept when they belong to a published package, and followed through re-exports with the same graph. A type or top-level function is published when one of its export names is on that surface; a `module` type only when an entry exports it whole (the entry itself or `export *`), so its members inherit it. `packages` is not emitted. Types are `class` (a class with a method), `data` (fields only: interfaces, object type aliases, classes without methods), `interface` (a type that a class of the project `implements`), `enum` (TypeScript enums and unions of literals; `cases` are the names) and `module` (one per file); fields carry `private` (modifier or `#`); `supertypes` are the project types an `extends` or `implements` clause names, resolved by symbol; an interface that precedes a class of the same name is merged into the class, which is the declaration described; types and functions inside `namespace`/`module` blocks are collected with the namespace path in their ids (`src/a.ts#Outer.Cart`, `src/a.ts#Outer.make`), and are exported only when every namespace around them and the member itself are exported and `mutable` (the field holds a fresh array, set, map or object, and is not `readonly`). A callable is a function, method, constructor or accessor (also a `const` function or a callback passed at module level, which is an entry); closures are folded into the enclosing callable. Ids carry no line or column: a static member is always `<type>.<name>(static)` and an instance member `<type>.<name>`, and a module-level callback is `<file>#<callee name>@<n>`, its ordinal among the callbacks of that name in the file, so inserting a line above changes no id. A name declared twice in one scope keeps the first id and gets `~<n>` on the later ones. Interface method signatures and abstract members are callables too, with a signature (`params`, `returns`) and no body facts, so a call through an interface or abstract-typed receiver resolves to them (an overloaded signature is left out). Only the public members (not `private`, `protected` or `#`) of an exported class are `exported`. Parameter and return types come from the declared annotation (TypeScript or JSDoc), normalised without generics or `| undefined`, and are the project type id when the type is a project type (`src/b.ts#Builder`) and the type text otherwise; inferred returns are not read. Calls are resolved by symbol (`getSymbolAtLocation` plus `getAliasedSymbol`, through imports, aliases and re-exports): a call whose target is unknown, a union receiver, or an overload set with several declarations is left out rather than guessed, since each fact is a claim a rule may act on. `references` lists the project callables used as values (an identifier or `obj.member` that is not called, compared, assigned to or used as a receiver, except through `call`, `apply` or `bind`), plus those held by a top-level `const` of the project the callable reads, one level deep and resolved with the same rules as calls; a `module` type's `references` are what code outside any function calls or uses as values. A read of a project accessor (`c.items`, not assigned to and not called) is a call to the accessor's callable, with `mutation` when the value is mutated, the same as `c.items()`. `cleanup` is set only when the call's own function (the nearest closure) is the one that holds the `finally` or the `using`, so a callback inside a `finally` closes nothing. `calls[].mutation`, `cleanup`, `forward`, `paramUses`, `ownFields`, `returnsField`, `decisions` and `reads` follow `shared/rules/facts/schema.mjs`. A decision subject that is both a field of a project type and of an enum or literal-union type is named by the field form (`<type id>.<field>`). Entries are `main`, a framework default export (`policy.frameworks.defaultExports`), overrides and members of an implemented interface, decorated members, `[Symbol.*]` hooks and callbacks.
- **Public contract usage.** Declaration identity keeps unrelated same-name methods and test-only calls from counting as consumers, so results are stable for a differential gate.
- **Test honesty config.** `eslint-config/test-honesty/` adds the test-area entries of `eslint.config.mjs`: `noInlineConfig`, the suppression-only mode of `no-comments`, the weak-matcher list for `vitest/no-restricted-matchers` and the rules of `eslint-plugin/test-*`. The setup files come from `setupFilesIn()`, which reads the text of the project's `vitest.config.*` or `vite.config.*` and takes the string literals of `setupFiles` and `globalSetup` that end in a code extension; a setup file named by a computed value is not seen. Imports are matched against the source roots like `no-internal-module-mocks` does, so a path alias is not resolved.
- **Pinned Vitest options.** `vitest.quality.config.ts` overrides the project's `retry`, `bail`, `allowOnly`, `passWithNoTests`, `dangerouslyIgnoreUnhandledErrors`, `testNamePattern` and `update` (`'none'`: a missing snapshot fails), and applies the layered execution deadlines from `tools.tests.timeouts` when configured. `unstubEnvs` and `unstubGlobals` are not pinned, since they would undo setup-file stubs before each test. Per-project options and the coverage-hint plugin are repeated in each `test.projects` entry because projects do not inherit the root: inline entries get them directly, function and promise entries once they resolve, and path or glob entries are expanded the way Vitest does it (a config file, or a directory with or without one; `!` patterns exclude, `node_modules` is skipped) into inline projects with `extends` set to that config file and `root` to its directory. The file is copied into the analyzed checkout on its own, so it imports nothing from the package. It loads Vite's `loadConfigFromFile` from the Vite that the project's Vitest resolves (`createRequire` on `vitest/package.json`, then a dynamic import of that Vite's `dist/node/index.js`, checked by `isVite`; Vite 5 to 8 ship that ESM entry, while resolving `vite` with `require` would pick Vite 5's CommonJS entry, whose namespace has no named `loadConfigFromFile`), not as a bare `vite` import: pnpm's isolated `node_modules` does not hoist `vite` next to the project, so a bare import fails coverage and mutation on every pnpm project that lists only `vitest` (`vitest-config-pins.test.mjs` runs the copied file in such a layout and with a Vite whose `require` entry has no named exports).
- **Ignore comments.** The same config strips `istanbul`, `c8` and `v8 ignore` hints (replaced by spaces, so positions stay) in a Vite `pre` transform. Stryker reads files from disk and analyzers share one checkout while ESLint reads the same files, so `Stryker disable` comments are not rewritten; the `Ignored` mutants they produce are enforceable findings instead.
- **Coverage run output.** The coverage run uses the `dot` reporter and the `json` reporter (`--outputFile.json=<file>`): the JSON file has no unhandled errors, and a timeout appears in it only as `Error: STACK_TRACE_ERROR`, so the runner reads the `Unhandled Rejection` / `Uncaught Exception` / `Unhandled Error` sections of stderr (header lines of `⎯`, the originating file in `This error originated in "<file>" test file`) and takes the timeout duration from the assertion.
- **Test runners.** `preset/test-runner/` picks the runner (`detect-runner.mjs`) and parses its command from the test script (`script-command.mjs`); `adapters/test-runners/` runs it and turns its output into one report shape, the Jest/Vitest JSON (`numTotalTests`, `testResults[].assertionResults[]`), which `quality-areas/reporting/test-report.mjs` reads for every runner. Long options are matched without dashes and case (`--outputFile` is `--output-file`); short ones exactly, because Mocha's `-R` (reporter) and `-r` (require) differ only in case. The coverage and mutation analyzer versions name the runner and its version, so switching runners changes the cache key. `node:test` and Mocha coverage: `NODE_V8_COVERAGE` plus `v8-coverage/loaded-sources.cjs`, preloaded with `--require` and passed on through `NODE_OPTIONS` (Mocha starts a child process for Node options, which inherits the environment but not the parent's arguments). `--import` would make Node load the main entry through the ESM loader and skip `require.extensions` hooks such as `ts-node/register`. The preload keeps an inspector session and records the source V8 compiled for each project script when it is parsed, skipping copies equal to the file; the `node --test` parent has no inspector, so there it only sets `NODE_OPTIONS`. Hook-based capture was tried first: a `module.register` load hook sees the code only when it is registered after the loader, and `module.registerHooks` crashed Node 22 on CommonJS loaded from ESM. `executed-script.mjs` picks the recorded copy whose length matches V8's script range and maps it back through its source map; for files no loader rewrote that type stripping rejects (enums, namespaces, parameter properties), it uses Node's transform mode with a source map. It drops a BOM and skips scripts with a URL query (module mocks). `mapped-nodes.mjs` drops AST nodes with no mapped segment in them (an `if` up to the end of its test, a function up to its body), the `__name(...)` and `obj.x = x` statements loaders add, and an enum function's fallback branch; these are loader helpers that the lookup would otherwise pin to line 1 or past the end. `merge-coverage.mjs` merges child processes and workers by type, line and order on the line, because columns differ by loader. With that, tsx, ts-node and Node's type stripping give the same functions and branches per line; a `ts-node` target below ES2022 is raised through `TS_NODE_COMPILER_OPTIONS`, keeping `useDefineForClassFields`. Mocha adds the `spec` of `.mocharc` and `package.json` to the files on its command line and only drops repeated strings, so a test file nitpicker passes that a `spec` glob also matches is collected twice; a serial run requires it once, but `--parallel` hands each copy to a worker, and two workers load and run it twice. The Mocha adapter therefore always passes `mocha/mocha-files.config.mjs` with `--require`, a root hook plugin with no hooks that wraps `Mocha.prototype.run` to keep each file once in first-seen order (the console capture resolves Mocha with its `loadMochaClass`). Jest's `--runTestsByPath` reads the script's own arguments as paths too and keeps repeats, so a rerun leaves out a file the script already names. Vitest gets no files outside a rerun, where they only filter its `include`, and `node:test` resolves the script's patterns itself and passes one set of paths, so neither runs a file twice.
- **Slow tests (TypeScript).** `adapters/slow-tests/slow-tests.mjs` reads the `duration` (ms) of each `assertionResults[]` entry of the report `runCoverage` already has, so every runner is covered by the one report shape and `slow-test` is reported by the `coverage` analyzer (`tool` must be the analyzer name). `test-calls.mjs` parses the test file with the TypeScript compiler and turns each `it`/`test`/`specify` call into a key (the `describe`, `suite` and `context` titles plus its own, joined by spaces, as a regular expression where `.each` placeholders and non-literal title parts are `.*`) and the call text with whitespace collapsed; a reported full name is matched against those keys, preferring keys with no dynamic title. The head policy's `thresholds` and `exceptions` are read with `readHeadPolicy()`, so they are already part of the cache key. `exclude` is `layout.isExcluded`. Only `passed` assertions count (`report-runs.mjs`). The findings carry `tool: 'test-runtime'`, which the analyzer lists in `reportedTools` (`quality-areas/analyzer.mjs`, `preset/checks/test-execution.mjs`) so `process.mjs` accepts it. A run over the smaller budget is timed again by `rerun-timings.mjs`: `runProjectTests` gets `options.rerun = { files, namePattern }`, and each runner adapter then runs just those files, selected by an anchored name pattern (Vitest and Jest `--testNamePattern`, `node --test-name-pattern`, Mocha `--grep`), with one worker and `uncoveredRun` (no coverage instrumentation, no coverage output). `runCoverage` awaits `readSlowTestFindings`, so the rerun happens inside the coverage analyzer. Changes to the seam files: `test-run.mjs` gains `testTimeoutMs()` (the 10 s `TEST_TIMEOUT_MS` plus the larger slow-test budget, used by the four adapters instead of `TEST_TIMEOUT_MS`), `workerCount()` and `uncoveredRun()`; `vitest.mjs`, `jest.mjs`, `node-test.mjs` and `mocha.mjs` use them and honor `options.rerun`; `quality-areas/runners/tests.mjs` awaits the findings. Integration tests are decided in `integration-tests.mjs` from the layout's test roots, as for Java: a folder name counts only below a test root.

- **Signature rules.** `eslint-plugin/rules/signatures/` holds `no-tri-state-boolean`, `no-falsy-default`, `no-parameter-mutation` and `no-compatibility-shim`. `no-falsy-default` and `no-parameter-mutation` read types from the type checker (`parserOptions.project`) or, in the JavaScript preset, from JSDoc; `parameter-owners.mjs` is the shared walk from a changed expression to the parameter it belongs to, with the exemptions (rest parameters, a rebinding to a copy before the change, `reduce` accumulators and accumulator defaults, immer drafts, and functions that are not exported or are private members); the method tables are in `analysis/mutation/mutating-methods.mjs`.

- **Security sink rules.** `eslint-plugin/rules/security/` holds one folder per rule (`sql/no-sql-string-building`, `hash/no-weak-hash`, `tls/no-disabled-tls-verification`, `http/require-http-timeout`, `deserialization/no-unsafe-deserialization`), set by `securityRules` in `eslint-config/policy/security-facts.mjs` (called from `productionGuardRules`) and listed in `strict-rules.mjs`. A later block of `productionPolicyConfigFor` turns the five off in test support files: the reuse rules' non-production folders (`structure/reuse/reuse-paths.mjs`: `__mocks__`, `__fixtures__`, `fixtures`, `stories`) and story globs, plus `test-support`, `.storybook`, `mocks` and `setupTests`-style files; the other production guard rules keep their scope. `securityFactsFor` reads project facts once per ESLint run from the analyzed root (like `routeHandlers`): a browser framework in `package.json` (`react`, `preact`, `vue`, `svelte`, `solid-js`, `@angular/core`) turns the `fetch` check off, the first tagged-template SQL library (`@prisma/client`, `drizzle-orm`, `kysely`, `postgres`, `slonik`, `@databases/pg`, `sql-template-strings`) is named first in the SQL message, the installed js-yaml major (`node_modules/js-yaml/package.json`, else the declared range) turns on the js-yaml 3 `load` check, node-fetch below 3 (or unknown) lets `timeout` count, and `git grep --no-index --exclude-standard` over the project (skipping `node_modules`) finds production files that set `<axios import>.defaults.timeout` to a non-zero value or call undici `setGlobalDispatcher` with a `headersTimeout`/`bodyTimeout`/`connectTimeout`; those pass `projectDefaults: ['axios' | 'fetch']`, which covers the axios default instance and global or undici `fetch` in every module. The rules otherwise read one file at a time, so a helper in another module is caught where its text is built or where its option is written, not where it is called. Shared value resolution is `security-values.mjs`: a `const` chain, a conditional or `??` fallback, a parameter default, a `CONFIG.key` lookup (also through `Object.freeze`), `CONST[key]` with a non-constant key (every value of a `const` object or array), a `for (const x of CONST_LIST)` binding, `toLowerCase()`, and through `member-values.mjs` an `enum` member, a `readonly` class field or `static readonly` field (fixed) and a mutable field or parameter-property default (one possible value). `imported-names.mjs` binds modules: `import`, `require`, `require(x).default`, `import { default as x }`, `import x = require(x)`, `await import(x)`, a namespace alias (`const sandbox = vm`) and aliases of an imported function (`const make = crypto.createHash.bind(crypto)`, `const { createHash: make } = crypto`). In `no-sql-string-building`, `sql-inert.mjs` classifies each interpolated value (constant text, a number shape, an index parameter of an array callback, `+` of inert parts, a template of inert parts, `join` of an inert array and text builders such as `repeat` and `slice`), `sql-arrays.mjs` decides when an array holds only inert text (a literal, `map` or `Array.from` with an inert callback, `fill`, or a local array whose every `push`, `unshift`, `splice` and element write adds inert text; with types a `number[]`), and `sql-sinks.mjs` names the consumer (query call, raw builder method, TypeORM clause on a `createQueryBuilder()` chain, a receiver named like `qb` or typed `*QueryBuilder`); `sql-composition.mjs` applies the SQL-context test per sink. With type information (`parserOptions.project`), `no-sql-string-building` also accepts a `number`, `boolean` or union of plain string literals in an interpolation, and `require-http-timeout` tells a URL argument from an options argument, a `Request` from a URL and a callback from options; without it both fall back to the syntactic reading (a callback is a function, a name like `resolve`, `cb` or `onResponse`, or an identifier after an options object). The rules do not read other modules, so a project helper that wraps `fetch` or `createHash` is judged where it calls them.
- **Renamed-identifier clones.** No preset runs PMD CPD (the Maven build passes `-Dcpd.skip=true` and the Gradle init script has no CPD task), and CPD could not cover the other languages anyway: PMD 7.7.0, 7.16.0 and 7.28.0 accept `--ignore-identifiers --ignore-literals` for `typescript` and `ecmascript` and neither lexer applies them (`TypeScriptCpdLexer` is ANTLR, `EcmascriptCpdLexer` a Rhino token stream, both emit every token image as written), and the `pmd-kotlin` (`KotlinCpdLexer`, ANTLR, whose filter only skips `package`, `import` and new lines) and `pmd-python` (`PythonCpdLexer`, JavaCC) lexers do not read the flags at all. A TypeScript or Python project also has no JVM or build tool to resolve PMD and its jars. So TypeScript, JavaScript, Python, Kotlin and Java (on Gradle and Maven: `runJavaDuplicates` passes the `.java` files by default, `runKotlinDuplicates` the `.kt` and `.kts` files, and `readJavaFindings` passes the project or Maven module root so the renamed report is read) run jscpd a second time over a normalized copy, all through `shared/renamed-clones/`. `withNormalizedTree` writes the listed source files to a `renamed-*` directory under the caller's report folder (the report root for Java, Kotlin and Python, `reports/quality/duplicates` for TypeScript), registered through the `temporaryPaths` the caller passes (`trackTemporaryPath` and `untrackTemporaryPath` from `core/tooling/temporary-files.mjs`, since the shared layer imports nothing outside it) so an interrupted run removes it too, under the same project-relative paths (generated files, by `@generated`, `DO NOT EDIT` and the generator headers of `generator-marker.mjs`, are left out) with every name, string, number and comment rewritten to a fixed token and every line break kept, so jscpd's line numbers are the original ones. `normalize/typescript.mjs` uses the TypeScript compiler's syntax tree (the project's own `typescript` peer, passed in, since `shared/` imports no tool package): imports and re-exports, `interface`, `type` and `enum` declarations, field declarations without logic and literal-only objects and arrays are blanked, and JSX markup (tags, attributes, text, and `{name}` or `{a.b}` expression holders) is blanked too, so two components that only share markup structure have no tokens in common while handlers, conditions and `.map` calls inside the markup stay. `normalize/scanned.mjs` does the same for Python, Kotlin and Java from a small scanner (`scanner.mjs`: comments, strings with templates, text blocks and raw forms, numbers, names): import and `package` lines, `__all__` and literal-only lists, dicts, tuples, `listOf` or `mapOf` calls, Java `List.of`, `Map.of`, `Arrays.asList` calls and `= { ... }` array initializers, and Kotlin `when (x) { ... }` bodies made only of data are blanked; a qualified name (`Status.NEW`, the counterpart of a TypeScript property access) and Kotlin's `->` count as data, as TypeScript counts names and property accesses. The run uses 100 tokens and 5 lines (`RENAMED_SETTINGS`), the Java CPD threshold; jscpd's 50-token run stays for exact copies. A renamed clone is reported as `duplicates/duplicate-clone` with an identity starting `renamed:` that hashes its token shape (so editing a name or literal in a legacy copy keeps it), the message of every `duplicate-clone` (so it names `exclude` as the way to lift it, and `clonePartnerPath` reads the pair from the identity without the prefix), and it keeps the descriptive `duplicate-clone-size` lines and tokens of the plain clone it replaces; a plain jscpd clone that covers the same two ranges is dropped (`withoutRenamedCopies`), as are clones that overlap their own copy, clones made only of declarations (`boilerplate.mjs`, matched on the original lines: fields, accessors, Python enum members `NAME = value`, Kotlin constructor parameters with a type whose default calls a function, Java fields, constructors and getters) and clones that only repeat one or two statements (`repetition.mjs`: the normalized fragment, spaces removed, holds a run of at least three copies of one unit of at most 120 characters and the rest of the fragment, with one unit kept, is under half of it), which is what DTO mappers, enum `when`/`switch` tables, argument lists and `if` ladders that differ only in names and literals look like once names are one token. TypeScript runs the second jscpd with its config in the copy, a relative `--config` path and `path: ['.']`, so names come out project-relative: with an absolute config path jscpd matches each ignore glob (`reports/**`) against any folder of the absolute path, which would drop every file of a copy that lives under `reports/`. The TypeScript `duplicates` check has a two-minute timeout for the two runs. Both runs pass their config by a name relative to the scanned folder and keep names relative (`absolute: false`; the plain run scans `.` with a `pattern` naming the layout roots), because jscpd matches its ignore globs against the names it reports: with absolute names a checkout under a folder called `build`, `dist`, `reports`, `target`, `generated` or `coverage` lost every file. TypeScript also drops a renamed clone between two test files that defines no named function, as for plain clones, and points two test files at a helper or a table-driven test.
- **Fan-out bounds.** `eslint-plugin/rules/concurrency/unbounded-fan-out.mjs` reports `Promise.all`, `allSettled`, `any` and `race` (also through a local alias of `Promise`) over a mapped, spread, pushed or `Array.from` collection when the per-element work reaches a remote or message call. Client recognition sits behind `ioKind(call, context)` in `fan-out-io.mjs`, which returns `remote`, `message` or nothing, so another recognizer with the same kinds can replace it; its tables (`client-modules.mjs`, `io-origin.mjs`) follow an import, `require` or function-level `await import()` to a table of HTTP, RPC, queue and cloud clients (a method list per client), through constructors, factory calls, `this.field` and type annotations; a call it cannot trace is never reported. The HTTP module lists and the global `fetch` check live in `security/http/http-modules.mjs`, shared with `require-http-timeout`, so the two rules cannot drift. Helpers are followed up to three calls deep. A bound (`bounds.mjs`) is a limiter (p-limit, p-queue, Bottleneck, p-throttle, async-sema, async-mutex, limiter, p-map with `concurrency`) created outside the worker and used inside it, held in a local, a class field, a constructor assignment or a parameter property, or imported from a project file under a limiter name; or a fixed collection (a non-empty literal, also behind `as const`, `satisfies` or `!`, the values of an `enum`, `Object.keys` of one, a slice with an explicit size that reads no `.length` or `.size`, a chunk helper with a size, or a `for…of` chunk variable); or a worker pool sized by a count (`Array.from({ length: n })`, an indexed `for` up to a name), where `sites.mjs` turns a count read from `xs.length` back into a fan-out over `xs`. A constant that no limiter reads is not a bound. Database clients are left to the per-item I/O rules.

## JavaScript preset (`languages/javascript/`)

The JavaScript preset reuses the TypeScript pipeline; `languages/javascript/preset/` holds only what differs.

- **Preset channel.** `resolveHeadPolicy` copies `preset` into the head policy file only for `javascript`, so the TypeScript policy and its cache key do not change. `readLayout` turns it into `layout.javascript` and the extension lists every runner reads from the layout: `extensions` (`ts`, `mts`, `cts`, `js`, `mjs`, `cjs`; no `jsx` or `tsx`, so a `.jsx` file is neither a source nor a test file), `entryExtensions` (the same, `js` first) and `formatExtensions` (the Prettier patterns). `presetAnalyzers` returns `javascriptAnalyzers()` (same checks, built from the JavaScript layout) and `applyLayout` rebuilds from `resolveLayoutFor(headPolicy)`. `javascript-preset.mjs` is hashed into every analyzer whose config it shapes.
- **Generated configs.** The `tsconfig.quality.json` and `tsconfig.stryker.json` rewrites add `allowJs` and `checkJs` (the first also `noEmit`); with a project `tsconfig.json`, or a `jsconfig.json` when there is none, they extend it. `knip.json` gains `.js/.mjs/.cjs` entries and config files, and `jscpd.json` the `javascript` format next to `typescript`, because jscpd's `typescript` format reads only `.ts` and `.mts`. Stryker's TypeScript checker reports a mutant that fails `checkJs` as a compile error, so JavaScript mutants are killed by types as TypeScript ones are.
- **ESLint dialect.** `eslint-dialect.mjs` rewrites each strict config entry whose rules change: the original entry is kept for `.ts`, `.mts` and `.cts` files and a copy with the dialect rules is added for `.js`, `.mjs` and `.cjs` files (the `files` patterns are intersected with the extension glob, so the entry's own scope is kept). The copy drops the type-syntax rules, `@typescript-eslint/no-require-imports` and the `no-restricted-syntax` selectors that name a `TS*` node, lets the property-assignment selector of `domain/` skip `module.exports` and `exports.x`, passes `jsdocTypes`, `jsdoc` or `commonJsExports` to the four rules that map to JSDoc or CommonJS, and `no-var`, `prefer-const`, `prefer-rest-params` and `prefer-spread` are added (typescript-eslint enables them for TypeScript files only). The mapped rules keep their severity and still accept an exception. The dialect is applied before exceptions.
- **JSDoc parser.** `jsdoc.mjs` accepts one grammar per allowed tag (type in balanced braces, then a name where the tag has one) and reports any other text as prose. A `@type` is a cast only when the comment is directly followed by `(`. `any`, `*`, `null` and the `?T`, `T?` and bare `?` forms are looked for outside string literals and object keys. `unknown` is reported in `@param`, `@property` and `@prop` anywhere, and in the other type tags only inside braces or the parameter list of a function type, which is where the TypeScript rule finds a property or parameter.
- **CRAP is computed here.** `crap/measure.mjs` (`measureCrap`) parses each source file with the TypeScript compiler (`functions/` finds every function-like node, its own complexity without nested functions, its display name and its source), reads the `coverage-final.json` the project's test run writes (`istanbul/`: statement and branch spans, `fnMap` entries, attribution of statements and branches to the innermost function by span) and applies `crap = ccn^2 * (1 - coverage)^3 + ccn` with coverage the lower of statement and branch coverage. `@barney-media/crap-typescript-core` and the module-load hook that widened its `.ts`/`.tsx` suffix list for JavaScript are gone. The identity (`function:<name>:occurrence:<n>:source:<hash>`, ratchet key `function:<name>:occurrence:<n>:crap`) is unchanged, so legacy CRAP findings stay baselined. `crap-equivalence.test.mjs` runs `measureCrap` on a corpus (`crap/fixtures/corpus`: TypeScript constructs, CommonJS, ESM and plain JavaScript, with a coverage file) and compares the whole result to goldens (`expected-ts.json`, `expected-js.json`) that were produced by the library; a differential run against it on thousands of functions gave no difference. Two deliberate differences: `.mts` and `.cts` files are measured (the library skipped them through its `.ts` suffix check), and a coverage entry is matched to a file by its resolved, lower-cased path, without the library's module-root fallback and without grouping the files by their nearest `package.json` (the order of functions within a file, and of files, is the library's for a project with one manifest). Absent values are `undefined`, so the report JSON leaves out the keys the library wrote as `null`; nothing reads them (`normalizeCrap` reads `percent` and `crap` as numbers), and `crap-equivalence.test.mjs` compares the golden with its nulls removed. JavaScript is parsed as TypeScript, which is why `.jsx` is not supported.
- **Constants in `quality-areas-runner.mjs`.** The module-level `invokedPath` block runs the runner on import, so the constants it uses are declared above it. Declared below, they were still in their temporal dead zone when a failing test reached the failure formatter, which crashed the coverage check instead of reporting `tests-failed`.

## Java preset (`languages/java/`)

Gradle runs once and yields findings for all tools.

### Java report directory

`gradle -I languages/java/gradle/nitpicker.init.gradle -Pnitpicker.reportDir=<dir> nitpickerJava`. `collectJavaFindings` makes `<dir>` a fresh temporary directory outside the project and removes it afterwards, so a build script that writes to `build/nitpicker` changes nothing nitpicker reads. When the root build (`gradle.parent == null`) has evaluated its projects, the init script writes `<dir>/modules.json`: `[{ key, path, dir }]`, one entry per project that applies the `java` plugin, in both presets (the Kotlin JVM plugin applies it too), `key` `root` for the root project and `module-<path>` otherwise, the path without its leading `:`, with `_` written `_u` and `:` written `_c` so two paths never share a key, `dir` the project directory relative to Gradle's current directory. Each project writes its reports to `<dir>/<key>/`; without `nitpicker.reportDir` (a manual run, the e2e tests) to `<project>/build/nitpicker/`. Every path in a report and every root in `manifest.json` is relative to Gradle's current directory, so a subproject's paths carry its folder. `readReportModules` (`adapters/gradle-reports/report-modules.mjs`) throws when the list is empty or a listed project has no `manifest.json`; findings are read per project, and a finding with path `.` of any project but the root gets `@<project path>` appended to its identity, so adding a project does not change the root's identities. A project without tests still gets a JaCoCo report: `nitpickerJacocoEmptyData` writes an empty execution data file that `jacocoTestReport` always reads, so its methods are reported as uncovered; a project without main classes may have none. Errors never name the temporary directory. Projects of an `includeBuild` composite build and `buildSrc` are separate builds: the init script returns before configuring any project of a build with a parent (`gradle.parent != null`), so they are not analyzed and none of their configurations change. A plugin build (`pluginManagement { includeBuild("build-logic") }`, `buildSrc`) has its classpaths resolved while the root build loads its plugins, before its project evaluation listeners have finished; applying JaCoCo, Checkstyle and the tool configurations there mutated `testImplementation` after resolution, a deprecation warning on Gradle 8 and an error on Gradle 9. A caller that needs the reports afterwards passes `reportDirectory` to `collectJavaFindings`, which then neither creates nor removes it. File limits, coupling, inline suppressions, Spring configuration and duplicates run once over the merged manifest. Coupling (`builds/project-wide.mjs`, shared with Maven) writes the `jdeps.txt` and `jdeps-classes.txt` of every project that has them into `<dir>/project/`, and reads them in place only when the root project is the only one, so a one-project build keeps its identities. A project's names depend on its folder alone, never on the number of projects: in the merged files a class of a project other than the root, even a lone one, is written `<folder>/<class>` (`<folder>/(root).<class>` in the default package), so its package is `<folder>/<package>`; an edge target is the class of the source's own project when it declares it, else of the one project that does, and an edge whose target several other projects declare is dropped. Each class's file is looked up in its own project's source roots and passed to `readCouplingFindings` as `files`.

- **Project tool configs** (`languages/java/adapters/tool-config/`, with the Checkstyle files in `checkstyle/`). `tools.pmd` and `tools.checkstyle` with `"project"` in `precedence` layer the project's file over nitpicker's instead of replacing it. `toolConfigChoices` returns `{ project, projectWins }` per tool (`projectWins` is false only when `"nitpicker"` is listed before `"project"`); `toolConfigProperties` turns them into `-Pnitpicker.pmd.nitpickerRuleset`, `-Pnitpicker.pmd.projectConfig`, `-Pnitpicker.pmd.testRules`, `-Pnitpicker.checkstyle.projectConfig` and `-Pnitpicker.checkstyle.mergedConfig`. `layerPmdRulesets` (`pmd-layers.mjs`) writes `pmd-nitpicker.xml` (nitpicker's ruleset minus the rules the project names or defines) and `pmd-project.xml` (the project's ruleset, where a rule nitpicker also configures is the overlay of the project's elements on nitpicker's definition, and a whole-category reference becomes single references to the rules nitpicker left out of that category), because PMD keeps the first reference of a rule and applies a ruleset's `exclude-pattern` only to a ruleset loaded on its own; it also clones `UnitTestShouldIncludeAssert` with the measured `java.assertionHelpers` appended to `extraAssertMethodNames`. `layerCheckstyle` (`checkstyle-layers.mjs`) merges modules by name (the `id` only chooses among several nitpicker modules of one name; a project module without an id merges into all of them), keeps the project's `SuppressionFilter`s and drops its inline suppression filters; for Gradle, `runJavaPreset` first runs the `nitpickerCheckstyleConfig` task with `-Pnitpicker.checkstyle.exportConfig` (nitpicker's config, from the pinned Checkstyle jar), merges it in process with `mergeCheckstyleFiles` (`checkstyle-merge.mjs`) and passes the result as `-Pnitpicker.checkstyle.mergedConfig`; the init script sets `configDirectory` to the head checkout's config folder; Maven calls it in `writeCheckstyleSetup`. Without a project file nothing is written and the tasks use nitpicker's files as before.
- **Report files** (in each project's report directory): `pmd.sarif` (PMD over every source set, SARIF 2.1.0, plus `spring-ruleset.xml` when the compile classpath has `spring-context` or `spring-boot`), `spotbugs.sarif`, `format.txt` (`--dry-run` of the formatter that runs, google-java-format or palantir-java-format: one file needing a reformat per line; empty when no formatter runs), `jacoco.xml`, `checkstyle.xml` (main) and `checkstyle-<source set>.xml` (every other source set), `archunit/*.xml` (JUnit XML of the project's `*ArchTest`/`*ArchitectureTest` classes; `-Pnitpicker.archunit.includes` overrides the patterns), `spring-arch.json` (nitpicker's Spring architecture rules, Spring projects only; see Spring architecture rules below), `structure.json` (`StructureMetrics.java`: per method `signature`, `parameterTypes`, `start`, `end`, `lines`, `statements`, `maxControlNesting`, `parameters`, `complexity`, `privateEmptyConstructor`), `pit/mutations.xml` (absent without main classes), `test-results/<task>/TEST-*.xml` (the JUnit XML of every checked test task that ran, copied by `nitpickerJava`; the checked tasks are `test` and the `Test` tasks `check` depends on, found by `checkedTestTasks` in the init script, and a task the build skipped (`enabled`, `onlyIf`) counts as not run, so `ranTestTasks` keeps its stale reports out; `jacocoTestReport` merges the execution data of the tasks that ran, each checked task has its own `nitpickerPit<Task>` run (merged into `pit/mutations.xml` by `nitpickerPit`) and, besides `nitpickerArchunit`, a `nitpickerArchunit<Task>` run; read for slow tests, and Surefire and Failsafe write the same format), `jdeps.txt` and `jdeps-classes.txt`, `manifest.json` (tool versions, `moduleKey`, `sourceRoots`, `testSourceRoots` = every source set but main, `resourceRoots`, `spring`). With `-Pnitpicker.kotlin=true` also `detekt.sarif`, `ktlint.sarif` or `ktfmt.txt`, `kotlin-metrics.json`, `kotlinc.json` (the kotlinc lint compile: `exitCode` and the compiler's `output`), and the `kotlin*` manifest keys.
