A tool that appears in the environment changes the launched command and is probed again, but a package installed behind an unchanged command (`pytest-django` in the same virtualenv) is not seen until a new process, and `process.env` is not part of the key. `python` has the versions of ruff, black, coverage and the test runner, `mutation` those of mutmut, pytest and pytest-django (`pytest-django@installed|missing`, since `-o django_find_project=false` depends on it). The configuration hash covers the analyzer name, the `python` settings, every non-test, non-fixture file under `languages/python/`, the transitive imports of `python-runner.mjs` (followed from the source, so a file the runner starts to import cannot be forgotten) and, through `styleFiles`, the project config files that can now apply: `pythonConfigFiles` (`pyrightconfig.json`, `pyproject.toml`, `mypy.ini`, `setup.cfg`, `.mypy.ini`, `tox.ini` and the `extends` chain) for `python`, and `mutationConfigFiles` (`pyproject.toml`, `setup.cfg`) for `mutation`, read from `policyRoot ?? projectRoot`.
- **Tool errors.** Each step (`ruff`, format, tests plus `coverage json`) fails independently and all failures are reported, one finding each, rule `<step>-failed` (`tests-failed` is the TypeScript rule), identity the rule, path `.`. When any step failed no other finding of that analyzer is returned, except that failing tests the runner names (`tests-failed`) keep them (see Red base). A test run that exits 1 without naming a failing test, or whose only failures are collection errors, is `tests-not-run`. An error without a `rule` property is `python-failed` (`mutation-failed` in the `mutation` part, whose only step is mutmut), with the analyzer as tool.
- **Format output.** `ruff format --check --diff` is parsed by its `--- path` / `+++ path` header pairs, because ruff's summary format differs between versions; black's by its `would reformat` stderr lines. Exit 1 without a parsed path is an error. Black exits 123 and ruff 2 when a file cannot be parsed; that is tolerated only when every `error:` line is a parse error (black words it `cannot format <path>: Cannot parse` or `cannot parse for target version`), because ruff check already reports the file. `exclude` globs go to ruff as `--extend-exclude` and to black as one `--extend-exclude` regex (`^/<glob regex>(/.*)?/?$`, black matches `/<relative path>`, with a trailing `/` for directories).
- **Test runner.** Decided from the project (`python-test-runner.mjs`), never from importability: a silent fall back to `unittest` runs none of a pytest project's tests and exits 5 or 0. `pytest` with a failing import probe is `environment-failed`. One probe prints the pytest version and whether `pytest_cov`, `xdist`, `pytest_rerunfailures` and `pytest_django` are importable; `--no-cov` and `-n0` are added only for installed plugins, since an unknown option is a usage error (exit 4). `-o cache_dir=<report dir>` replaces `-p no:cacheprovider`, which broke `--lf` and `--sw` in `addopts`. `unittest discover -s tests` needs `-t .` when `tests/` is a package, or relative imports in it fail. Detection (`python-test-runner.mjs`, with the `manage.py` lookup in `python-django.mjs`: the project root, then its direct subdirectories, and the tests run from that directory; mutation is skipped (`django-subdirectory`) for a subdirectory, because mutmut's module names, which it derives from the path below the root, would not match the ones the tests import) checks an explicit pytest configuration or dependency first, then Django (`manage.py`), then nose2, then pytest-style test files, so a nose2 or Django project with `def test_` functions is not run by pytest. Django runs `manage.py test --noinput`, which sets the project's own `DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE`; `unittest discover` would import the tests without settings and fail them all. For mutation, mutmut's pytest gets `-o python_files=test*.py` when the runner is not pytest, and `-o django_find_project=false` whenever pytest-django is installed: otherwise pytest-django finds the real `manage.py` above `mutants/` and puts the unmutated project first on `sys.path`, so every mutant is reported as `NoCoverage`. The project's coverage `[run]` `concurrency`, `patch`, `plugins` and `omit` and its `[report]` `omit` are carried into the generated rcfile (`python-coverage-config.mjs`, read with `parseIni` of `mypy-config.mjs`, which like configparser keeps a continuation across blank and comment lines), with relative `omit` patterns and `source` made absolute; per-process data files are combined before `coverage json`. `manifest.json` `testsRan` is false on exit 5 or `Ran 0 tests`; the reader turns it into the enforceable `python/no-tests-ran` finding, which a comparison reports only when head lost its tests (a per-snapshot analyzer cannot see the other side, so it is a finding and not a tool error).
- **Slow tests.** `languages/python/adapters/test-runs/`. The test command writes the JUnit report (`pytest_timings.py` or `unittest_timings.py`, see `python-test-runner.mjs`). `slowCandidates` in `slow-tests.mjs` reads it (passed cases only), classifies and change-checks each test over the smaller budget (a file or test that cannot be read becomes a descriptive `slow-test-not-timed` notice, not a thrown error), and is shared by two callers. The `retimeSlowTests` step (`slow-retime.mjs`, after `test` in `runPythonTools`; `testSeconds` is on the tool context) runs the candidates once more through `execute` with `python <test command>` (no `coverage run`), the JUnit path swapped for `rerun-junit.xml` and a node id (pytest) or `-k <dotted id>` (unittest) per test. `readSlowTestFindings` (called from `python-reports.mjs` with `testsRan` from the manifest) reads the same candidates, replaces a time with the one in `rerun-junit.xml` when that run passed it, and builds the findings with `slowTestFindings` of `shared/rules/slow-tests/`, so the message, budgets and exception hint are those of Java and TypeScript. A missing JUnit report after a run that ran tests is a notice. The findings carry `tool: 'test-runtime'`, which `python-analyzer.mjs` lists in `reportedTools`.
- **Ruff target version.** `--target-version` is the lowest of `requires-python`, poetry's `python` or `python_requires` (minimum 3.8, also the default); `--config` makes ruff ignore the project's own `requires-python`, so without it version-gated rules such as `B905` flag code that cannot use the fix. Not passed to `ruff format`, which reads the project's config.
- **Coverage.** Run through a nitpicker-owned `coveragerc` (branch on, parallel off, `source` the source roots; `writeCoverageConfig` in `configuration/coverage-config/python-coverage-config.mjs` reads the project's file with `readCoverageSettings` from the snapshot, or from the policy root when `tools.coverage` lists `"project"`, and then adds the opted-in run keys and, unless nitpicker is listed first, the project's `exclude_lines`/`exclude_also` to its `exclude_lines` and `partial_branches`/`partial_also` to its empty `partial_branches`, regexes split one per line) with `COVERAGE_FILE` in the report directory; `coverage json --fail-under=0 --ignore-errors`. Ingestion (`coverage-json.mjs`): `line-coverage` from `summary.covered_lines / num_statements` per file, `uncovered-function` for a function (not the module-level `""` entry) with statements and no covered line, identified by its qualified name. A file with no statements has no metric.
- **Structure and CRAP.** Details in `docs/python-preset.md`. The structure step lives in `python-structure.mjs`; it reuses the shared reader (`readStructureFindings` in `shared/ingest/structure-report.mjs`) and passes a Python function-text hash (common indentation removed, not every line trimmed, because indentation is syntax). `python-crap.mjs` reads the same `structure.json` and gives its functions to `ingestPythonCrap` (`ingestCrapRows` in `shared/ingest/lizard-crap.mjs` underneath). There is no external complexity tool: `structure_metrics.py` computes the cyclomatic complexity from the `ast` module. Definition (McCabe, the decision points of the other presets; a lambda is an entry of its own like a TypeScript arrow function, and the `for` and `if` clauses of a comprehension count): 1 plus one per decision point in the function's own body, where decision points are `if` and `elif`, a conditional expression, `for` and `async for`, `while`, each `except` handler, each `case` of a `match` but a guard-less `case _` (the `default` of a `switch`, which ESLint's `complexity` rule and the Java and Kotlin metrics do not count either), each extra operand of `and` and `or`, and each `for` and `if` clause of a comprehension. `else`, `finally`, loop and `try` `else`, `with` and `assert` add nothing; comprehensions belong to the enclosing function; lambdas (named after the scope and the name they are assigned to, else `<lambda>`, with `lambda: true` and `statementLine`), nested functions and the methods of nested classes are entries of their own and add nothing to it. A lambda has no coverage.py entry or line of its own, so its coverage is that of the statement that creates it (100 when the statement ran, 0 when it did not). Control nesting treats an `elif` as the same level as its `if`, like `else if` in the TypeScript and Java analyzers; an `else:` block holding an `if` is one level deeper. The entries also carry `definition` (the `def` line) and `bodyStart` (first body line); a function whose body starts on its `def` line is left out of CRAP, because its statements share the line that runs at import. Coverage entries are joined to functions by qualified name (the names coverage.py reports) and line range, not `start_line`, which older coverage.py versions do not report. coverage.py keeps one entry per qualified name, so a function no entry covers (property setter pair, `singledispatch` `_`, conditional definitions) is scored from the file's per-line and per-branch data over its own range, minus nested functions.
- **Changed-branch coverage.** `coverage-branches.mjs` reads the same `coverage.json` (`executed_branches` / `missing_branches` as `[from, to]` arcs, a negative `to` leaving the scope), groups the arcs by `from` line and hands one entry per line to `branchCoverageFindings` (`shared/rules/branch-coverage.mjs`, minimum 95). Its tool name is `coverage-py`, not `coverage` (the TypeScript analyzer's), and `TOUCHED_CODE_RULES` in `shared/policy.mjs` lists `changed-branch-coverage` for `coverage-py`, so an uncovered branch on a changed or added line is new even when the base had it. Identity is `branch-source:<hash of the enclosing def/class chain and the whitespace-normalized line>`, with `#n` counted per path and hash among failing lines; a missed arc from a `case` line that leaves the `match` (an exit, or a line indented less than the `case`) is described as no case matching, with the `case _:` + `assert_never(subject)` form; the chain is derived from the indentation of earlier `def` / `class` lines, because the coverage JSON has no scope for a branch. The `coveragerc` also sets `omit` (test files), a fixed `exclude_lines` (`if TYPE_CHECKING:`, the `__main__` guard, `@overload`, `raise NotImplementedError`, a `def` whose signature, wrapped or not, ends in `: ...` on the same line as its colon, and the `case _:` arm whose only statement is `assert_never(name)`, `ASSERT_NEVER_ARM` in `exhaustive-match.mjs`; both are multi-line patterns that need coverage.py 7.6, written with `[ \t]` so they never cross a line where the source does not) and an empty `partial_branches`, so no pragma and nothing in the project's own coverage config applies. coverage.py gives no arcs for `try` / `except` / `finally`, so those are not gated. Java's `jacoco-branches.mjs` numbers identical lines per file, without a scope.
- **Mutation.** `python-mutation.mjs` (the `mutation` part) checks that mutmut is installed and 3.x from the same `importlib.metadata` probe, runs `mutmut run` with `PYTEST_ADDOPTS` extended by `-n0` when the pytest probe saw xdist (mutmut runs pytest itself, and the `-n` of the project's `addopts` makes it fail to collect stats and report every mutant as uncovered), and removes head's `mutants/` afterwards. On Windows it returns a descriptive `mutation-skipped` finding instead of running. pytest exit 5 under mutmut ("failed to collect stats. runner returned 5") yields no mutation findings, since `python/no-tests-ran` covers it; any other "failed to collect stats" adds the `also_copy` line. `python-mutation-data.mjs` reads `mutants/**/*.meta` (`exit_code_by_key`) with the generated and the original source; `languages/python/ingest/mutmut.mjs` splits the generated file into function blocks by indentation (a closing bracket at the `def` indent continues a wrapped signature), diffs `x_f__mutmut_orig` against `x_f__mutmut_N` on tokens, drops mutants whose changed original lines lie inside an arm that `ASSERT_NEVER_ARM` matches (the same pattern the coverage settings exclude, turned into a JavaScript `RegExp` by `javascriptPattern`; unreachable once the type checker passes), reports a mutant at the line of its first changed token (an arm-removal mutant at its `case` line), and hands `Survived` and `NoCoverage` (exit 0; 5 and 33) to the shared `mutantFindings`, plus a `mutation-summary` metric per status over all parsed keys. Keys exist but none matches `x…__mutmut_N` is a thrown error naming the supported range (`SUPPORTED_MUTMUT`, the one place the range is written in code). Identity `symbol|original→mutated#n`, `n` counted per status and file among equal keys in mutant order (a same-named function in another module has its own count); the line comes from locating the original block in the real file. Exit 1 with "could not find any test case for any mutant" is accepted and turns the unchecked (`null`) results into 33. `writeMutmutConfig` (`project-tools/mutmut/mutmut-config.mjs`) always writes nitpicker's `[tool.mutmut]` into the snapshot (mutmut reads the pyproject first, so a `setup.cfg` section stops applying), keeping from the project's table only `also_copy`, the process-isolation keys and, for source roots of `.`, `source_paths`; `mutmutSettings` applies the project's whole table over nitpicker's only under `tools.mutmut.precedence` with `project` first. The `do_not_mutate` of a generated `[tool.mutmut]` is the test patterns plus each `exclude` glob and `<glob>/*` (mutmut matches with `fnmatch` against the project-relative path, where `*` also crosses `/`). Head's `mutants/` is removed after reading; base's stays so `python-mutation-reuse.mjs` can copy its `.meta` files (only those) into head when the name-status diff from the base commit to head lists nothing but added or modified non-test `.py` files under a source root, and only those of modules outside the changed files' reverse import closure (`import-scan.py`: a Python `ast` script run in the project's environment over head's source roots, edges to every module an `import`, `from … import` or relative import can name, files that do not parse have none; a failed scan reuses nothing).
- **Ruff selection.** `nitpicker-ruff.toml` lists the stable codes of the `RUF`, `G`, `N`, `PT`, `S` and `TRY` groups one by one. `ruffConfigFor` (`python-ruff-config.mjs`) runs `ruff rule --all --output-format json` in the environment, keeps the stable rules (`isStableRule`: not preview, not removed), and writes the toml without the selected codes the installed ruff does not know (a prefix like `E4` is kept when a known rule starts with it), so ruff 0.9 to 0.16 run one file; when the listing fails it uses the toml as it is. The lint step adds `--config 'lint.flake8-pytest-style.<option> = <value>'` flags from `pytestStyleFlags` (the set options read from the policy root, the measured ones from the checked code), and the rules step calls `ruffConfigFor` too, so `--show-files` and the lint agree on nitpicker's own scope. When `tools.ruff` lists `"project"`, `ruffLintConfig` (`project-tools/ruff/ruff-lint-config.mjs`) writes `nitpicker-ruff-layered.toml` to the report folder instead and the lint passes that: `readRuffLayers` returns the project's `extend` chain from the policy root, `ruff-project-lint.mjs` turns it into a lint view per file (top-level legacy lint keys under the `[lint]` table, paths re-rooted from the policy root to the checked snapshot, bare names kept as ruff matches them by basename), and `ruff-settings.mjs` lets ruff resolve the selection: `ruff check --show-settings --config <file> --no-force-exclude <report dir>/nitpicker-ruff-probe.py` (with `--no-preview` under `["nitpicker", "project"]`), run once for nitpicker's toml and once for the project's file from the policy root, prints `linter.rules.enabled` as `name (CODE)` lines and `linter.per_file_ignores` as `absolute_matcher` / `basename_matcher` / `negated` / `data` groups; the codes in parentheses are what ruff enables after the `extend` chain, specificity, the pydocstyle convention and its redirects. A per-file pattern whose basename matcher has no separator is written back as that basename (ruff matches it by file name), any other as its absolute matcher re-rooted from the policy root to the snapshot. The layered toml selects exact codes: nitpicker's enabled set plus the project's extra codes (minus the codes of nitpicker's own `ignore` list under `["nitpicker", "project"]`) and per-file ignores reduced to the extra codes. With the project first, the option tables merge key by key with arrays joined, and `target-version` (then no `--target-version` flag), `indent-width`, `builtins`, `preview`, `src`, `namespace-packages` and `per-file-target-version` come from the project. With nitpicker first, `ruff-option-linters.mjs` keeps only the project's option tables whose linter (its code prefixes, such as `pep8-naming` to `N`) has none of nitpicker's codes, and only `src`, `namespace-packages` and `indent-width` of its top-level keys. `--line-length` is always passed: the followed `python.lineLength`, or the project's value when it is first and agrees with the followed one (or none is followed).
- **Ruff identity.** The SARIF goes through `prepareSarif` (`shared/ingest/sarif.mjs`) with the message text as the subject, so identity is `fp:nitpicker/subject=<message>`; ruff messages name the symbol, and repeats in one file get `#n`.
- **Type checker choice.** `detectTypeCheckers` scores each checker from the live checkout's hint files: a config (`[tool.mypy]`, `mypy.ini`, a `[mypy]` or `[mypy-...]` section, `[tool.pyright]` or `[ tool.pyright ]`, `pyrightconfig.json`) counts 2 and a declared dependency (a name anywhere on a line, so inline arrays count) 1; the higher score wins, and a tie or no hint at all probes `mypy --version` then `pyright --version` then `basedpyright --version` in the environment (`resolveTypeChecker`). basedpyright is a third entry: its config is `[tool.basedpyright]` (or `[ tool.basedpyright ]`), its dependency is the name `basedpyright` or the `basedpyright-pre-commit` hook. basedpyright also reads `[tool.pyright]` and `pyrightconfig.json`, so once it scores above 0 those no longer count for pyright and only a declared `pyright` dependency can still pick pyright. The probes run `mypy`, `pyright`, `basedpyright` in that order. The choice is read from the live root so both snapshots run the same checker, and it is part of the tool versions in the cache key.
- **mypy run.** `mypy --strict [--enable-error-code deprecated] --explicit-package-bases --show-error-codes --show-column-numbers --no-error-summary --no-pretty --hide-error-context --no-color-output --cache-dir <report dir>/mypy-cache <source roots>` with `MYPYPATH` set to the snapshot's import roots (mypy does not read `PYTHONPATH`); `--enable-error-code deprecated` is added when `mypy --version` reports 1.14 or later (an older mypy rejects the unknown code); the pyright config sets `reportDeprecated: error`. Exit 0 or 1 is a result, anything else `types-failed`. `--config-file` is a file generated in the report directory (`mypy-config.mjs`): the project's `[mypy]`, `[tool.mypy]` (pyproject) or `mypy.ini` section reduced to `plugins` (file plugins made absolute), `mypy_path`, `python_version`, `namespace_packages`, `custom_typeshed_dir` and per-module `ignore_missing_imports` and `follow_imports`, plus nitpicker's `exclude` as one regular expression; everything that skips or weakens checking is dropped, so no project setting can turn the gate off. `--python-executable` is the environment's `sys.executable`, because mypy otherwise introspects its own interpreter and misses the project's packages. Lines are matched as `path:line[:col]: error|warning: message  [code]`; notes and the summary are ignored. Identity is the message, `#n` per (file, rule).
- **pyright and basedpyright run.** basedpyright has pyright's CLI and `--outputjson` shape (`ingestPyright` with tool `basedpyright`) and is run the same way: `basedpyright --outputjson --project <report dir>/pyrightconfig.json`. Its modes are `off`, `basic`, `standard`, `strict`, `recommended` (its default) and `all`; the config forces `strict`, which has pyright's meaning, because `all` adds opinionated rules such as `reportAny`, `reportUnusedCallResult` and `reportImplicitOverride`. It reads `pyrightconfig.json`, `[tool.basedpyright]` and `[tool.pyright]`; it does not read a `basedpyrightconfig.json` (1.40), so nitpicker does not detect or extend that name either. A baseline (`.basedpyright/baseline.json`) is looked up next to the config and does not apply to files outside that directory, so none applies from the report directory. basedpyright 1.40 honors `# pyright: ignore` only (not `# basedpyright: ignore`), which `no-inline-suppression` already bans. A generated `pyrightconfig.json` in the report directory (`extends` the project's `pyrightconfig.json` or a `pyproject.toml` with `[tool.pyright]` (`[tool.basedpyright]` too for basedpyright), `typeCheckingMode: strict`, `enableTypeIgnoreComments: false`, `ignore: []`, `executionEnvironments: []` (a project's per-directory environments can switch rules off), `exclude` nitpicker's globs relative to the config (plus pyright's defaults) in place of the project's, `include` the source roots, `extraPaths` the import roots). `include` has to be relative to the config file (pyright ignores absolute entries with a warning); `extends` and `extraPaths` may be absolute. The JSON is read from the first `{` because the pip wrapper can print a notice first; only the first line of a message is the identity.
- **Project tool layers.** `languages/python/adapters/project-tools/` (`project-tool-layers.mjs`: `toolLayers(context, tool)` returns `{ optedIn, appliesProject, nitpickerFirst, configRoot }` from the shared `precedenceLayers` for `tools.pyright` (also basedpyright), `tools.mypy` and `tools.mutmut`; `ignored-notices.mjs` prints the one `Ignoring ...; opt in with ...` line per tool through `pythonStyle().summary`; `requireProjectConfig` throws the shared missing-config error). Without the opt-in only environment keys are read: `pyright/pyright-project-config.mjs` copies `venv`, `venvPath`, `pythonVersion`, `pythonPlatform`, `stubPath`, `typeshedPath`, `extraPaths` and the root, `extraPaths`, version and platform of each execution environment (following `extends`, JSONC parsed by `shared/jsonc/jsonc.mjs`, `[tool.pyright]` or `[tool.basedpyright]` TOML), and `mypy-layers.mjs` keeps per-module sections only for modules the project does not own (the entries of the import roots and of the `mypy_path` directories: directories, `.py` and `.pyi` files, `-stubs` packages). Under the opt-in with `project` first the project's keys overlay nitpicker's strict config; the project's `include`, `exclude` and `extraPaths` are merged with nitpicker's and its other keys replace them. The config is read from `policyRoot`, but `pyright-project-config.mjs` re-roots the source paths (`include`, `exclude`, `extraPaths`, `stubPath`, an environment's `root`) onto the snapshot's own `projectRoot` and leaves `venvPath` and `typeshedPath` in the head checkout; under the opt-in it searches the parent directories up to the first `.git`. A config that fails to parse comes back as `{ file, files, unreadable }` and `requireProjectConfig` reports it as unparsable; `files` (also the `extends` chain) feeds `pythonConfigFiles`. The mypy project `exclude` is left as written and nitpicker's globs become `--exclude` arguments. mypy then gets `strict = true` in the file and no `--strict` flag, because a flag overrides global keys but not per-module sections, and the file value lets project keys override it.
- **Python libraries.** `languages/python/rules/libraries/`: `project-libraries.mjs` `detectLibraries(projectRoot)` returns `{ database, logging, validator, validators }` from the declared dependencies (runtime before development; `DEFAULT_LIBRARIES` when the project shows none), using `library-tables.mjs` (`VALIDATORS` with the package names, the import roots the boundary guards and the call that parses, `LOGGERS`, `DATABASES` with the placeholder style of the SQL hint). `dependencyNames` and the detection read through `languages/python/libraries/python-dependencies.mjs`, the one declared-dependency reader (the dependency findings, the framework allowances and the pytest collection options use it too), with its parts in `dependencies/` (`requirement-names.mjs`, `requirement-files.mjs`, `setup-py.mjs`): `readPythonDependencies(projectRoot)` returns `{ runtime, development, dependencies, complete, declaresDev, manifest }`, where `runtime` and `development` are sorted normalized names (a package in both counts as runtime) and `dependencies` lists each declaration with its group and file, read from `pyproject.toml` PEP 621, optional dependencies, dependency groups, Poetry (its `main` group is runtime), uv, pdm and hatch, a `Pipfile`, `requirements*.txt` (without constraints files, following `-r` includes inside the project, reading the `.in` beside a compiled file or only its direct entries), `setup.cfg` and the literal lists of `setup.py`, decoding a BOM or UTF-16 (`readProjectText`); `requirementName`, `normalizeRequirementName` and `isDevelopmentGroup` are exported with it. The detection runs when the report is read (`readPythonFindings`) and when the rules step writes its options, where `boundaryRoots` (the import roots of every declared validation library) is how `pydantic-boundary` knows what to guard.
- **Rules script.** `nitpicker_rules.py` is stdlib-only and needs Python 3.10 or later (every Python still supported upstream; the file itself parses on older ones, so below 3.10 `main` writes one `rules-skipped` entry with the interpreter version, which `ingest/rules-report.mjs` turns into a `python-rules/rules-skipped` tool error, instead of a traceback); it takes a file list, a report path and an options file (`rule-options.json`), parses each file once with `ast`, finds comments with `tokenize`, and works in two phases. `index/project.py` first builds a `Project` over all the files (the names each defines, suffixes, module names and relative imports, one context per file, the test files, a memo, and `locate` for the definition a call refers to), so a rule can follow a call into another file; an absolute name resolves by suffix only when its first segment is a project directory that is not in `sys.stdlib_module_names` and not installed outside the working directory (`importlib.util.find_spec`), so `import json` never lands on a project `json.py`; `index/scopes.py` holds what a module or function binds at the top level (definitions, also under top-level `if` and `try`, imports, aliases, annotated and class-body assignments; a later unconditional binding replaces an earlier one, a function's parameters and locals shadow module names, an import inside another function does not count, a local definition wins over an import in the other branch of an `if`, and both sides of a `try` count); then every module of `checks/` (comments, declarations, shell, state), `security/` (`sql/`, tls, hashes, timeouts, loading), `discovery/` (test collection, helpers), `environment/`, `errors/` (`errors.py` and `handling.py` for `swallowed-exception` and `blind-suppress`; `cancellation/` for `cancellation-not-propagated`: `errors.py` asks first (`handlers.py`), and a handler inside an `async def` that catches `asyncio.CancelledError`, `trio.Cancelled` or `anyio.get_cancelled_exc_class()` (resolved through the imports and single plain assignments of the enclosing scopes, in `names.py`, so a shadowed name or a local module is not a backend) is judged only by this rule, never by `swallowed-exception` or the log rule; `paths.py` computes the set of outcomes of the handler body (falls through, propagated, lost, maybe and unknown; `break` and `continue` only inside a loop of the handler), reports when a falling or lost path exists, and lets nested `try/except`, `except*`, a `match` without a catch-all and a `with` around a `raise` fall through as maybe, which keeps the following statements analysed but never reports on its own; `suppress_finding` applies the same rule to `contextlib.suppress`; `tasks.py` (`awaits_cancelled_task`) exempts a quiet handler of a `try` whose body only awaits tasks that a dominating earlier statement of the function cancelled (name or attribute chains, `gather`, cancel loops), simulating the statements of the enclosing blocks in order so a rebinding drops the cancel; `logged_errors.py` for `catch-log-without-exception`: a `warning`, `error` or `critical` call in an `except ... as name` body that uses the caught name and neither keeps the traceback nor sits in a handler that re-raises, nested handlers included, through the shared `is_log_owner`, asked by `errors.py` only for a handler it finds handled, so a handler that only logs is reported once, as `swallowed-exception`), `boundary/`, `silent_tests/` (`unasserted.py` for `test-without-assertion` over each test function of a collected test file, fixtures excluded, with `calls.py` for what counts as an assertion and `helpers.py` for the project's assertion helpers, found in a first pass over all files as the names whose `def` in test or support code asserts in its own body and that end the assertion-free tests of at least three test files), `smells/` and `honesty/` runs its `check(context)` (`checks/common.py` holds the shared helpers: import aliases resolved to dotted names, test, domain and boundary path detection, logger call detection; `smells/io_index.py` is the index of input and output functions that `io-in-loop` uses; `effects/` holds the effect summaries of project functions: `names.py` and `calls.py` classify one call as input or output, a clock read, a random value or an external effect at import (`external`), `resolve.py` resolves a call to every production function or method it can mean through the `index/scopes.py` bindings (imports, aliases, relative imports, `__init__` re-exports up to sixteen modules, `Class.method`, `self.method` in the same class, a class to its `__init__`; `follow` is the one re-export resolver, which `module-state-in-domain` uses too), and `summaries.py` builds, once per project, each function's direct effects from its own body (not nested functions, lambdas, decorators or defaults) and spreads them over the reversed call edges with a worklist, so a function is revisited only when a callee gains a kind, each kind keeps the first path found and recursion ends; a second summary without the `domain/` functions gives the path that stays out of `domain/`, and a shown path longer than seven steps keeps its first four and last two; `reach.py` reports domain calls into helpers outside `domain/` and `adapters/` (bare decorators included) with the helper's qualified name as subject, and `import_time.py` (`import-time-effect`) walks what a module evaluates at import; `environment/paths.py` finds the `pathlib` file methods, including on module-level `Path` constants that a function or class body reads; `security/resolve/` is the value resolution the five security rules share: `scopes.py` resolves a name to what it is bound to (in the function, the enclosing functions, the module or the project module it is imported from, with parameter defaults and loop targets of `enumerate`, `zip` and `.items()`), `classes.py` indexes each class once per project (class-body values, annotations, methods, `self.x` writes in any method, dataclass `field(default_factory=...)`, and subclass overrides of an attribute only for subclasses whose base resolves to that class) and resolves `self.x`, `cls.X`, `type(self).x`, `Class.X` and attributes of an imported project module, `calls.py` gives the effective calls (a `functools.partial` is checked where it is defined unless it is bound to a name that is called, then at each call with the bound arguments merged; also `getattr(x, "name")` and method aliases) and the entries of an options dict (a literal, `dict(...)`, `**options`, and writes in the same scope: `d[k] = v`, `update`, `setdefault`), and `booleans.py` answers whether a value can be `False` or `True` (an `or` can be `False` only through its last operand). Every index is built once per project in its memo, and a check resolves receivers only for the method names it watches, so a file costs one pass per check. `security/sql/` holds `check.py` (the SQL shape: statements including DDL and `MERGE`, clauses in SQL shape), `sinks.py` (query calls by name pattern, aliases, the raw builders and the wrapper index, a project memo of functions and methods that pass a positional or keyword-only parameter straight to a query call), `pieces.py`, `iterables.py` and `shapes.py` (each piece of a built query as text, inert or unsafe, allowlist guards and annotations) and `schema.py` (SQLAlchemy and Django metadata); `security/tls/` and `security/timeouts/` split the switches and client factories from the check, and `security/support.py` decides the test support files.) `signatures/` holds `tri-state-boolean`, `falsy-default` (`defaults.py`), `parameter-mutation` and `compatibility-shim`: `annotations.py` reads an annotation for a `bool` member (depth-bounded, so a deeply nested one cannot recurse without end, and an empty `Annotated[()]` is skipped), `classes.py` finds data mapped from outside (pydantic, SQLModel, Django and SQLAlchemy models, with the bases it can resolve), `scopes.py` gives comprehension variables and lambda parameters their own scope, and `copies.py` decides whether a rebinding of a parameter is a copy of it. A security check, a `signatures/` check, and a module that sets `GUARDED = True` (`effects/reach.py`, `effects/import_time.py`, `environment/domain_imports.py`), runs inside a guard: an exception in it becomes one `rules-check-failed` entry naming the check's `RULE`, the file and the error (a `python-rules/rules-check-failed` tool error), and the other checks and files still report; the other checks are not guarded, so a bug there still fails the run loudly as `rules-failed`. It writes `[{rule, path, subject, line, via?}]` sorted by path, line, rule and subject (`via` is the helper chain of a finding reached through project helpers, `caller -> module.helper -> ... -> effect`; `ingest/rules-report.mjs` shows it in the message instead of the subject and keeps it out of the identity, so a change inside a helper keeps the finding's identity), and beside it `rules-allowances.json` with `supportFiles` (the test support files whose security findings are dropped, ruff's included) and `allowed` (`{rule, path, line}` sites of ruff findings that the script proved to be an allowed form: `S303` on the digest of an HMAC or of RSA-OAEP/`MGF1`, `S506` on `yaml.load` with a loader deriving from `SafeLoader`), which `adapters/analysis/python-ast-rules.mjs` reads (`readAllowances`, empty when the file is missing); a subject repeated in a file is numbered `#n` by the ingester. Files that do not parse or tokenize are skipped, and the modules are imported inside `main` so the script writes no bytecode into the tree (the configuration hash covers every file under `languages/python/`). Messages are not in the script: `rules/hints/` owns every message (`rule-hints.mjs` assembles them), so they are testable without Python. The options file is how a rule reads a project fact that only JavaScript knows: `sourceRoots`, `testRunner`, the whole pytest collection configuration (`pythonFiles`, `pythonClasses`, `pythonFunctions`, `norecursedirs`, `testpaths`, `deselected`, `ignored`, `ignoredGlobs`, `keywords`; read by `pytest/pytest-config.mjs` from `pytest.ini`, `pyproject.toml`, `tox.ini` and `setup.cfg`), `testFiles` (the files pytest collects, which also decide the test-file exemptions of ruff's findings), and `entryFiles` and `entryModules` (`python.entryPoints` and the script entry points of `pyproject.toml` and `setup.cfg`, read by `libraries/entry-modules.mjs`), which make a file a composition root. Why these rules are heuristics and where they stop: a taint rule such as `unsafe-path-join` follows names inside one function only (six assignment steps), `handwritten-boundary-guard` knows the parsers it lists, and `io-in-loop` knows the HTTP, database and repository names it lists plus receivers named like a repository or client; each has fixtures of the correct form that must stay quiet, and a project with an unusual form lifts the rule for its path with `python.exceptions`. Ruff cannot express two allowed forms, so `rules/filters/ruff-allowed-forms.mjs` drops, after ruff runs, `PLR2004` findings for the numbers the TypeScript preset allows and `S602`/`S604`/`S605` findings for a literal command (ruff words them "seems safe"), the ruff findings on the sites the rules script listed in `allowed` (so `weak-hmac` reports HMAC-MD5 once and HMAC-SHA1 is allowed, found from the syntax tree, not the text), and ruff's security findings in the listed `supportFiles` (`rules/filters/test-support.mjs`). The script drops its own security findings in the same files: files under a test root (the pytest `testpaths`, `tests`, `test`), `conftest.py`, and the folder and file names the TypeScript preset's security scope uses, in their Python spelling, when they sit inside a test root or no production module imports them (`security/support.py`, a fixed point over the project's imports).
- **Design facts.** `languages/python/rules/design/` (`run.py`, `report.py`, `facts.py` and `project_index.py` at the top; `resolve/` for symbols, imports, `__all__` export lists, lookup, annotations and values; `support/` for the shared AST, scope and attribute helpers; `types/`, `callables/`, `calls/` and `decisions/` for the facts of each kind) produces `{ modules, types, callables }` of "Design facts" from the same `ast` parse and `Project` of the rules script: `main` calls `write_design` (`design/report.py`) when the options carry `designFiles` and writes `design-facts.json` beside `rules.json` (`{ "facts": ... }`, or `{ "error": ... }` when the producer raised), `design/run.py` runs it alone on a file list, and `analysis/design/python-design.mjs` reads it and calls `sharedFactFindings(facts, { language: 'python', ... })` from `python-reports.mjs`. `designFiles` (`analysis/design/design-files.mjs`) are the listed sources inside the source roots, minus the `exclude` globs and generated files; test files come in as `role: "test"` modules (`conftest.py` and the like included, through the test-file matcher of the rules) and give no types or callables. `published` marks the public surface of a published distribution: `designDistributions` (`analysis/design/design-distributions.mjs`) passes the rules script, as `distributions`, every folder of the analysed files that holds a `pyproject.toml` with `[project]` or `[tool.poetry]`, published when `[build-system]` names a `build-backend` and no classifier is `Private :: Do Not Upload`; in the deepest distribution of a file, `design/types/published.py` marks a module-level class, `Literal` alias or function whose name has no leading `_` (or is listed in `__all__`, when the module has one) in a module of its packages, with no part of the module path starting with `_`: every folder under the distribution's `src/`, a folder chain from the distribution root that ends in folders with `__init__.py` (namespace folders above them count, except `tests`, `docs`, `examples`, `scripts` and the like), and a single-file module at the root when the distribution lists it (`py-modules`, poetry `packages`/`include`, or a file named like the project). A name a public module of a published distribution imports or star-imports and does not hide behind `_` or `__all__` is published too, so `pkg/__init__.py` re-exporting from `pkg/_impl.py` publishes the class. Methods and nested classes count through their owner. A producer error, or facts the shared schema rejects, becomes the single tool error `python/design-failed`, never a silent pass. The producer resolves statically, never by importing code: imports (absolute by the exact dotted module name of a source root, relative, aliases, re-exports, `*` limited by the target's `__all__`, imports inside a function, `import a.b as c`), class bodies with the MRO (a base that is not a project class blocks the lookup of an inherited member unless it is a known transparent one), annotations (`Optional`, `X | None`, unions, quoted names, `Self`, `ClassVar`/`Final` wrappers; a union of several project types or a generic around a project type keeps its text), and the value of a name from its assignments only when every assignment of it agrees (instantiation, a call whose return is annotated, a parameter annotation, an `await` of an `async def`, `with ... as`). A call is a `calls` entry only when the receiver resolves to a project type that defines the member; `receiver` is the dotted key of the receiver with local aliases of `self` fields or parameters collapsed, a property read is a call of the accessor, `super().m()` is a call on `self`. Ids carry no line or column: a callable is `<file>#<qualified name>`, a property setter or deleter `<file>#<owner>.<name>(setter)` or `(deleter)` beside the getter's `<owner>.<name>`, a name defined again in the same file `<id>@<n>` (its ordinal among the definitions of that name), and an `@overload` signature is left out so the implementation keeps the plain id. Nested functions and lambdas are not callables but are folded into the callable around them, as the TypeScript producer folds closures: their calls, parameter uses, decisions, reads and mutations count for it (a class defined inside a function is not folded), `cleanup` comes only from a `finally` or `with` inside the closure itself, so a lambda in a `finally` closes nothing, a closure parameter is a binding of its name, so reusing an outer name leaves that name untyped (and a use of the parameter `opaque`), and the returns, `returnsField` and generator checks read only the callable's own body. `returns` goes through the same normalisation as a parameter type, so an annotated project return type (quoted, `Optional` or `Self` included) is the type id. A `Protocol` method and an abstract method are callables with their signature, so a call on a receiver typed as the `Protocol` or the ABC targets them. `cleanup` marks calls in a `finally` and the implicit exit of `with` and `async with` (a `finally` or `with` inside a nested function counts for the callable only when the function is called in it, so a closure that is defined and never called closes nothing; `design/calls/exitstack.py` treats `stack.callback(x.close)` and `stack.enter_context(x)` on a `contextlib.ExitStack` or `AsyncExitStack` held by a `with` as a cleanup call or a `with x`): a `with` on a project class adds, at its line, the unconditional `self.m()` calls (followed up to three levels) of the class's `__enter__`/`__aenter__` as plain calls and those of its `__exit__`/`__aexit__` as cleanup calls, on the receiver and on an `as` name the enter returns as `self` (`contextlib.closing(x)` is a cleanup `x.close()`), and `__enter__` itself gets the cleanup calls of its `__exit__`, because the `with` statement owns both halves. The dunders themselves are not listed, so a `with` block is not a repeated step sequence for `caller-protocol`, and the producer needs no `protocols` contract to emit them. `branch` (`design/support/branches.py`) lists the arms a call sits in, outermost first: `"<n>:then"` or `"<n>:else"` for the body or `orelse` of an `if` (an `elif` is the `if` in the `else` arm) or a conditional expression, and `"<n>:<case index>"` for the body of a `match` case (not its pattern or guard), where `n` counts the conditionals of the callable in pre-order, folded closures included; the implicit enter and exit calls of a `with` and the calls an `ExitStack` registers take the markers of the statement or call they come from. `mutation` is set for a call whose result is mutated in place (`append`, `extend`, `update`, `add`, `sort`..., `+=`, an item or attribute assignment) directly or through a local that is assigned once (also by `:=`, or through `x or []` and a conditional expression),; `returnsField` also names a mutable module-level variable or class-level field (`ClassVar` included) the callable returns, directly or through a local (`y = CACHE; return y`), from an awaited call when the callee is `async`, and not when the mutator resolves to a project method. `supertypes` are the direct bases of a class that resolve to project classes (a `Protocol` or ABC of the project included; library bases, `Generic[...]` and repeated bases are left out). A kind is `enum` for `Enum` subclasses and `Literal[...]` aliases, `interface` for a `Protocol` or `ABC` subclass, an `ABCMeta` metaclass or a class with an abstract method, `class` for a class with a method of its own (not a property, a validator or a neutral dunder such as `__repr__`), and `data` for the rest (dataclasses, `NamedTuple`, `TypedDict`, pydantic and attrs models); module-level state is the `module` type's fields (private unless `__all__` lists it; `mutable` when every assignment creates a mutable container and no function rebinds it with `global`; a `global` declaration that only reads the name rebinds nothing). `__all__` (`design/resolve/exports.py`) is read from module-level list or tuple literals of strings, `+=`, `append` and `extend`; a computed value, a non-literal argument or a change inside a block makes it unknown, and the underscore rule applies instead. `exported` follows it: a module-level name is exported when `__all__` lists it, or, without a known `__all__`, when it has no leading `_`. A callable whose analysis overflows the recursion limit is listed with its id, kind, owner and `exported` and no parameters or calls, and chained calls are evaluated iteratively, so a long builder chain does not overflow. `entry` marks a decorated function (decorators of `functools`, `abc`, `typing`, `contextlib.contextmanager` and `property`/`classmethod`/`staticmethod` are neutral), a callback passed to a call that is not a project callable, `main` of a file with a main guard, a `__main__.py` or a declared entry file, a function the main guard calls, every dunder except `__init__`, an override of a project method, and an override of a method of a known standard-library base class (`unittest`, `threading`, `json`, `logging`, `ast` visitors, `http.server` and the like). `decisions` are `match` statements and `if`/`elif` chains of `==`, `is`, `in` and `or` over one enum or `Literal` value or one field of a project type (when the field's own type is an enum or `Literal`, the subject is that type rather than `<type>.<field>`), and a comparison with `None` never counts; `reads` are the fields and properties read off a typed local, parameter or `self` attribute (`decided` lists those in a branch condition). `forward` needs at least one parameter passed on and never targets a constructor. `references` (`design/calls/references.py`) are the project functions and bound methods a callable uses as values (passed, stored, returned, inside a lambda), plus those in a module-level `dict`, `list`, `tuple` or `set` literal that it reads by name, one level deep; a module type's `references` are what its import-time code calls or uses (statements, decorators, default values, class bodies; not function bodies or `__all__`). It leaves out what it cannot type: `getattr`/`setattr`, decorators that wrap the call, monkey patching, `__getattr__`, `*args` forwarding, metaclass effects, and star-argument calls (a forwarded or used parameter on such a path is `opaque`).
- **Hints and exceptions.** `applyRuleHints` (both parts) appends the allowed form (when the rule has one) and the one exception sentence, `exceptionSentence`, to enforceable findings not on `.` of `ruff`, the type checkers, `python-rules`, `coverage-py`, `duplicates`, `coupling`, `import-linter`, `dependencies`, `test-runtime` and `mutmut` by rule id (a prefix for `PTH`; `no-import-outside-roots` is routed by its setting instead; `exception-sentences.test.mjs` fails on any other file that writes a route); an entry with `exception: false` carries its own advice instead; `allowed` and `describe` may be functions of `(subject, libraries)` (`allowed` also gets the finding's path, so `catch-log-without-exception` advises raising instead of logging in `domain/`), where `subject` is the script finding's subject (kept on the finding until `withHint` strips it) and `libraries` is `{ logging, validator, validators, pytestStyle }`, read from the project's dependencies and test code by `rules/libraries/` (`detectLibraries`, `pytestStyle`; `DEFAULT_LIBRARIES` when the project shows none), so one rule words its advice for structlog or loguru, pydantic, msgspec, cattrs or marshmallow, and the pytest style the project already uses (`pytestStyleFlags` also hands that style to ruff as `--config` flags; an explicit `flake8-pytest-style` table in the project's ruff configuration wins over the measured one); other findings pass through. Identity is computed before, so a reworded hint never re-baselines; `on line N` and `from line N` are stripped from identity subjects (`withoutLineNumbers`), so moved code keeps its identity. `python.exceptions` filters findings by rule id (exact, or a ruff prefix such as `PTH` for ruff findings) and path glob after hints; the ids are validated when the config loads (`python-rule-ids.mjs`: the ruff selection, the `python-rules` ids, pyright and basedpyright `report*` names, mypy code spelling) and before `exclude` and the legacy inventory; the base snapshot uses the base's config, like every other setting.
- **Runner arguments.** `core/tooling/runner-arguments.mjs` (`parseRunnerArguments(arguments, { runner, choices })`) parses the options the Java, Kotlin and Python runners share and names `runner` in its usage error. The allowed values of options a language owns come in as `choices`: Java passes `--preset` and `--kotlin-formatter` through `parseJavaRunnerArguments` (`languages/java/adapters/java-policy.mjs`), so `core/` does not import a language folder; the Python runner takes its own options out first and passes the rest with no choices.
- **Shared policy.** `exclude` and the legacy inventory are `configureJavaAnalyzer` / `applyFindingPolicy`, both language-neutral; `applyFindingPolicy` takes `inventoryTool` for the tool of a `baseline-expansion` finding. It matches an `exclude` glob or an exception against every file a finding is about: a `duplicate-clone` or `duplicate-clone-size` finding is about both files of its pair, the second read back from the identity by `clonePartnerPath` in `shared/rules/duplicates.mjs`.
- **Duplicates, coupling, import-linter.** More reports in the directory: `duplicates.json` (the `runJscpd` output shape of `core/tooling/jscpd/run-jscpd.mjs`; Python passes `together` to run once over all roots: jscpd scans `.` with a `--pattern` naming the roots (`rootsPattern` in `core/tooling/jscpd/skipped-folders.mjs`), so names come back project-relative and the skipped-folder globs never see the folders above the checkout, and reads it with its own `readDuplicateFindings`, which drops import-only clones and adds the renamed clones from `renamed-duplicates.json` (a second jscpd run over the normalized copy of `pythonSourceFiles`, without generated files); `runJscpd` always passes a nitpicker-owned `jscpd-config.json` so a project's `.jscpd.json` is never read), `imports.json` (`{ modules: [{ file, module, isPackage }], imports: { file: [{ level, module, names, typeOnly, guarded, local, line }] } }`: `guarded` marks a statement whose failure the code expects (see the unresolved imports in python-preset.md), `local` one inside a function or lambda: the module list comes from `python-sources.mjs`, the statements from `import-scan.py`, which only parses and never resolves) and `import-linter.txt`. Resolving imports to modules, packages and the stability graph is JavaScript (`python-coupling.mjs` into `buildCouplingReport` of `shared/rules/coupling.mjs`), so only the scan needs Python. `typeOnly` is true for a statement under `if TYPE_CHECKING:` (the flag is `TYPE_CHECKING` from `typing` / `typing_extensions`, as `typing.TYPE_CHECKING` or an alias, or the module-level `TYPE_CHECKING = False` idiom; one operand of an `and` counts; other `TYPE_CHECKING` names are plain names) and for the `else` branch of `if not TYPE_CHECKING:`; the `else` of `if TYPE_CHECKING:` is not. `collect` walks the tree with an explicit stack because a recursive walk drops a file with a 1000-branch `elif` chain or a 2500-term sum into `RecursionError`, which `scan` would skip silently. `importEdges` gives each edge `kind: 'runtime'` or `'type-only'`, an edge is type-only only when every statement behind it is, and `buildCouplingReport` computes every metric, package instability included, from all edges, and `computeCoupling` skips an edge of `kind: 'type-only'` only as the candidate dependency of a `stability-direction` violation; TypeScript `import type` edges get the same treatment through the same function (recomputing instability without them would let moving unrelated imports under `TYPE_CHECKING` create a violation elsewhere). import-linter counts them unless the project sets `exclude_type_checking_imports`. A file the scan cannot parse is skipped silently. The config the contracts come from is read by `readImportLinterConfig` (`analysis/import-linter/import-linter-config.mjs`) in import-linter's order (`IMPORT_LINTER_FILES`: `setup.cfg`, `.importlinter`, then `pyproject.toml`) as `{ file, session, contracts: [{ id, options }] }`, ini multi-line values as lists and TOML booleans as `True`/`False`; the runtime detection and `readToolConfigs` both use it, and `import-linter-changes.mjs` in `core/policy-changes/build-files/` compares the two sides key by key, per contract id. The import-linter text report is parsed in `ingest/import-linter.mjs`; its identity per chain ignores line numbers, and the order import-linter prints chains in is not stable, so tests must not depend on it.
- **Runner parity.** `test-runs/` holds the JUnit reader, the pytest option pins (`-o addopts=` and a filtered `PYTEST_ADDOPTS`), the test step and the `tests/tests-failed` and `test-runtime/slow-test` findings; pytest runs as `pytest_timings.py <pytest arguments>` (`pytest.main` with one plugin object: a `pytest_fixture_setup` hook wrapper sums the setup time of `scope == "function"` fixtures per item, which includes `setup_method`'s generated fixture and not `setup_class`'s, and adds it to the call report's `duration`, which `-o junit_duration_report=call` writes; `pytest_collection_modifyitems` appends a `nitpicker_marks` user property with the names of `item.iter_markers()`, so a `pytest.param` mark reaches only its case), and unittest runs through `unittest_timings.py <report> discover ...` (a `TextTestRunner` whose result times `startTest` to `stopTest` and writes the same report, with the class's module and qualified name as `classname` and the file and 0-based line of the unwrapped method's code, as pytest's `file` is where the function is defined), so both runners feed one reader. `slow-tests.mjs` takes the collecting file and class from the longest `classname` prefix that is a project file. `python-blocks.mjs` splits a file into logical lines (bracket depth, single and triple-quoted strings, backslash continuations, comment-only lines as blank) and returns each `def` block (decorators, signature, body) with its name, its `owner` (the enclosing class and function names) and its marks (its decorators, every enclosing class's decorators and class-body `pytestmark`, the module `pytestmark`; `withoutParamCalls` drops `param(...)` calls when no recorded marks exist), plus `classHeader` and `mentions` (the logical lines naming a test); the head block is the one at the reported line (a factory's inner function when the name differs), else the one with that name in the collecting class; a test is unchanged when the base file at its path, or else a base test file the head no longer has, has the identical block with the same owner, and, for an inherited or factory-built test, the collecting class's header and the lines naming the test are in the base too; Java's `test-times/inherited-tests.mjs` follows `extends` the same way for a test method its class does not declare; `size/` the folder-depth and class limits over the shared `shared/rules/size/` rules, with the Python defaults and `PYTHON_SIZE_RULES` in `shared/project-rules/size-rules.mjs` (folder depth: each module's package path after the leading namespace folders of its top-level tree, `namespaceDepths`: the index of the tree's shallowest package capped at the folder prefix all the tree's modules share, from the base for trees the base has and from the head otherwise, measured below the common prefix of the base's production modules, as Java's root; test files from `imports.others` are entries of kind `test` whose path drops the source root and the `tests`/`test` folders; class limits: `metrics/class_metrics.py` writes `kind` `protocol` (a `Protocol` base), `interface` (an `ABC`/`ABCMeta` class whose methods are all `@abstractmethod`), `record` (`@dataclass`, attrs decorators, `NamedTuple`, `TypedDict`) or `class`, no fields for the first two, no `@overload` stubs as methods, and `self.x` without entering nested classes; the inheritance walk is memoized per class, counts only resolved project bases and stops at protocol and interface kinds; a base that resolves to the class itself takes the import instead, and when Kahn's queue runs dry with classes left (a cycle and what inherits from it), the first one left is queued with the depth of its parents already measured, which breaks the cycle there and lets the walk go on); `libraries/generated-files.mjs` marks `*_pb2.py`, `*_pb2_grpc.py` (and `.pyi`), a generated-looking path (`generated/`, `_generated/`, `__generated__/`, `*_generated.py`) with `hasGeneratorMarker`, and any file with `hasGeneratorHeader`, which the source listing, the file limits and folder depth leave out (`shared/conventions/generator-marker.mjs`: `hasGeneratorMarker` tests the generic markers on the first five lines, and TypeScript's `markedGeneratedFiles` also gates it on `mayBeGenerated`; `hasGeneratorHeader` matches the exact lines protoc, gRPC Python, datamodel-codegen, OpenAPI Generator, protobuf-es, Thrift and the `Code generated by <tool>. DO NOT EDIT.` convention write against `headerLines`, the leading `#`, `//` and `/* */` comments and one module docstring, so it counts for any file name; `markedGeneratedFiles` reads the first 16 KB of every code file for it, and the evals' `detectGeneratorMarkers` flags either kind written into a file by hand); test files come from the pytest collection configuration (`testFileMatcher` in `python-sources.mjs` over `testFileOptions` and `isTestFile` in `configuration/pytest/collection-options.mjs`), the same as the rules use: `test_*.py`, `*_test.py`, the project's `python_files` except a pattern that matches the probe names `a.py`, `service.py` and `models.py` (then a file under a `testpaths` folder is a test instead), and `DJANGO_TEST_FILES` only when `isDjangoProject` (a Django distribution, `manage.py`, or `DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE` in the pytest options), which the coverage `omit`, mutmut's `do_not_mutate` and `nonProductionGlobs` follow too; `imports/` the cycle and outside-roots rules over `imports.json` (which also lists the non-production `others`); `dependencies/` the distribution scan, the dependency findings, the framework allowances and the packages a framework loads for the project (`impliedDistributions`: a detected framework plus a marker in the project's code, such as Django's `ImageField` for `pillow`); `languages/python/libraries/python-dependencies.mjs` the declared-dependency reader, with its parts in `languages/python/libraries/dependencies/`; `mutation/test-run/mutation-scope.mjs` the Python version skip and the failing-module exclusion, `mutation/mutation-markers.mjs` the `mutant-ignored` findings.
- **Project root.** `resolveProjectRoot` accepts a `nitpicker.config.json` as a project marker beside `package.json`, because a Python project has no `package.json`.
- **Fan-out bounds.** `rules/concurrency/` holds `unbounded-fan-out`. `remote.py` resolves a call receiver to a client factory through assignments, `with` targets, annotations and `self` attributes (`kind(call)` returns `remote`, `message` or nothing; one function, so another recognizer that returns the same kind names can replace this module's client tables). `elements.py` finds the per-element sites of `gather`, `wait`, `as_completed` and `create_task` (comprehensions, `map`, a list grown by `append` or `extend`, loops that spawn tasks) and `functions.py` follows project helpers up to three calls deep and decides whether a limiter (`asyncio.Semaphore`, `anyio.CapacityLimiter`, aiolimiter and the like) used in the helper was created outside it. The client factories come from `index/clients.py`, the one table `smells/io_index.py` reads too. A limiter is also one passed by the caller (`functions.py` binds the call-site arguments to the helper's parameters) or a parameter or import annotated with a limiter type, else matched by name. `bounds.py` accepts a fixed collection (a non-empty literal never grown, the members of a project `Enum`, a slice with an explicit size, a chunk helper or a chunk variable) and a count-driven `range`, while `range(len(xs))` stays a fan-out over `xs`. `fan_out.py` memoizes the reported sites per file (`reported_work`), and `smells/loops.py` skips exactly those calls and the count-driven worker tasks, so `io-in-loop` does not report the same site twice and still reports a bounded or awaited per-item call. The finding subject joins its fields with `\x1f` (`FAN_OUT_SEPARATOR` in `hints/ast-smells.mjs`), which cannot occur in source text. Database calls and unresolved receivers are never reported.
- **Task rules** (`rules/tasks/`, run by the rules script). `lifetimes.py` and `handed.py` implement `task-outlives-resource`: a walk over each function body that keeps the stack of enclosing task groups (a `with` item, or a variable assigned once from a group constructor), bound resources and `with res:` names, a record of local `def`s with the resources they capture (parameters and local bindings excluded), and the names a spawn call hands to the child; a task handle read again in the resource block ends the finding. `cleanup.py` and `shields.py` implement `unshielded-cleanup-await`: in a module with Trio or AnyIO cancellation evidence, the first checkpoint of a `finally` or `trio.Cancelled`/`anyio.get_cancelled_exc_class()` handler body that is not under a `shield=True` scope. `bindings.py` resolves a name assigned exactly once in its function or the module (shield scopes, shield constants, stored cancellation classes, group variables) and gives subjects their canonical dotted names and enclosing function. asyncio-only modules are left alone because asyncio cancels a task once.

## Go preset (`languages/go/`)

One analyzer, `go`, run as the `nitpicker go-analyzer` subcommand (`adapters/go-runner.mjs`) once per snapshot, so no runner script is an entry point of its own. `--go-settings` is the `go` config object as JSON, and the shared runner arguments (`core/tooling/runner-arguments.mjs`) carry the policy; `--policy-root` (the head checkout) is where the formatter choice and its golangci-lint options are read for both snapshots, so a change that adopts a formatter checks the merge base with it too. A `tool` directive in the snapshot's own `go.mod` runs that formatter with `go tool`; otherwise the pinned version is built into the cache. The child environment is the CLI's environment with `GOFLAGS` set to the project's `go env GOFLAGS` (which includes `go env -w` values) plus `-buildvcs=false`, and the pinned tools build with `GOWORK=off` and an empty `GOFLAGS`, `GOOS`, `GOARCH` from a neutral folder.

Steps (`adapters/go-steps.mjs`), each writing into a temporary report directory: `toolchain` (`go version`), `inspect` (`inspect.json`), `format` (`format.json`, the unformatted files per formatter), `vet` (`vet.txt`, the stderr and stdout of `go vet -json`: Go 1.26 writes the JSON to stdout, older releases to stderr, and compile errors stay on stderr), `goTest` (`test-events.jsonl` and `coverage.out`). A failing `toolchain` or `inspect` step stops the run; any failed step makes the snapshot's findings just its tool errors. `go-findings.mjs` turns the reports into findings, and `applyFindingPolicy` adds the exception routes: `vet` findings route as `<tool>/<rule>` (`GO_LINT_TOOLS` in `adapters/configuration/go-config.mjs`), the rest through the shared rules.

`inspect.json` (from `languages/go/inspect`, standard library only): `files` (`path`, `package`, `test`, `generated`), `functions` (`file`, `name` as `Receiver.Method`, `Function` or `Outer.funcN`, `start`, `end`, `lines`, `statements`, `maxControlNesting`, `parameters`, `complexity`), `types` (`file`, `name`, `kind` `struct`/`interface`/`type`, `start`, `end`, `methods`, `fields`), `tests` (top-level `Test*`, `Benchmark*`, `Fuzz*`, `Example*` functions with their spans) and `errors` (files that did not parse). The program walks the module like the go tool: it skips `testdata`, `vendor`, `node_modules`, folders starting with `.` or `_` and folders with their own `go.mod`. Slow-test retiming reads the merge base's metrics (`inspect-base.json`, from `--baseline-root`) to tell new and changed tests from untouched ones.

Tool binaries (`adapters/execution/go-tools.mjs`): `<cache>/go-tools/<name>@<version>/<name>` and `<cache>/go-inspect-<hash of the inspect sources>/inspect`, built into a `*.staging-*` sibling and renamed into place, so two concurrent runs never see a half-written binary.
